Trajectory Planning

11 Trajectory Planning

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why must every motion a robot follows already contain the stop it will probably never use?

A moving robot cannot pause its momentum while a learned policy computes the next piece of its motion. Each new plan must take over from motion already under way. A plan that matches only the arm’s position leaves a jump in velocity or acceleration, which the motors can meet only with a sudden step in torque. A real drive refuses such a step or converts it into vibration, wear, and a spilled load. A path that clears every obstacle can still ask for more joint speed, torque, or current than the body can supply, and the fastest timing along that path demands exactly the abrupt changes a drive cannot make. The next plan can also arrive late, because inference time has a long tail, and a machine that keeps moving while it waits spends the distance it needs to stop. Without trajectory planning, a correct decision about where to go becomes a command the body cannot follow, or motion that cannot be halted in the room that remains. Planning attaches timing, a smooth join to the present state, and a stop that runs without the proposer. Those three properties decide whether a command that crosses the causal boundary can still be brought to rest before its energy reaches something that cannot be undone.

Learning Objectives
  • Distinguish prediction horizon, executed prefix, renewal cadence, and evidence age when budgeting trajectory replacement
  • Map tool-space speed limits to joint rates through the manipulator Jacobian, explain why the mapping fails near a singularity, and bound an inverse-kinematics step there with damped least squares
  • Evaluate a timed trajectory and its time parameterization against actuator torque, current-slew, and voltage limits, including reflected rotor inertia (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\))
  • Synthesize closed-form quintic spline bridges, anchored at the projected seam state rather than a stale sampled pose, that eliminate acceleration steps and bound commanded jerk across replanning seams
  • Size a jerk-limited \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stopping suffix from deceleration and jerk bounds and verify stopping clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\))
  • Compare sampling-based search, trajectory optimization, and learned action chunking by the work each performs and the checks its output still requires
  • Construct trajectory records whose two-tier deadlines (high-rate chunk lease and hardware commitment cutoff) keep motion out of inevitable collision states (ICS) and support independent per-tick permission decisions
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

11.1 Continuous Trajectory Generation

The arm of the warehouse mobile manipulator is reaching for a mug that drifts along a moving conveyor when an inference pass is delayed by operating-system scheduling contention. The intent lease still names the mug as a valid target, but a lease grants a target, never a path. A target coordinate paired with an expiration timestamp specifies neither velocity nor acceleration, so it cannot tell the arm what to do while the next pass is late. The arm cannot freeze abruptly, and it cannot extrapolate blind setpoints into space no check has covered. Planning must supply a concrete, time-parameterized path, an admissible transition from the motion already under way, and a precomputed stop that runs even if the next inference pass never returns.

↩︎ The intent lease, whose expiry bounds the validity window of every trajectory, is established in The Intent Lease Specification and Contract.

Classical robotics split this problem into two stages. A search first finds a collision-free geometric path through a configuration space, and a time parameterization then assigns timing that respects kinematic and torque limits. Learned sensorimotor policies change both stages. Instead of searching a static, pre-mapped scene, a policy emits short chunks of future setpoints conditioned directly on live camera observations, and because it replans while the machine moves, each new trajectory must splice into motion already under way rather than start from rest. Neural inference also has variable, heavy-tailed latency, and the hardware keeps moving along its previous trajectory while it waits. Geometric clearance therefore does not settle a plan. A plan must also establish how long the proposed motion remains executable, how it joins the retiring trajectory, and how the machine comes to a controlled stop if later inference passes stall.

On this machine, the arm’s low-level joint tracking loop evaluates feedback and setpoints at 1 kHz (datasheet; see the Reader Guide); however, this high-rate servo clock does not specify the physical stopping response. Above this loop sits the Brain, where learned policies such as Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) (Zhao et al. 2023) or Diffusion Policy (Chi et al. 2025) process sensory observations on the host application processor to synthesize future reference paths.1 The machine’s chunk policy operates with an illustrative budget of 40 ms per pass at p99, vision included. Even so, memory contention, direct memory access (DMA) traffic, or thread preemption can stretch tail latency beyond 150 ms. The real-time controller therefore cannot block synchronously on policy execution; it requires both buffered nominal motion and an immediately dispatchable, pre-validated stop.

1 Action chunking representations: ACT and Diffusion Policy, the 2023-era chunk policies, carry no boundary-derivative constraints. Real-time chunking (Black et al. 2025) conditions each new chunk on the actions already committed, which makes a consistent join more likely without guaranteeing one, so the planner still proposes a transition bridge across each replanning seam and the permission path checks it before admission.

Black, Kevin, Manuel Y. Galliker, and Sergey Levine. 2025. “Real-Time Execution of Action Chunking Flow Policies.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).

↩︎ The kinetic energy a trajectory commits, and the stopping distance that energy demands, are established in Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay.

Whether candidate motions originate from sampling-based search, numerical trajectory optimization, or learned action chunking (section 11.6), the generator serves strictly as an unprivileged proposer. Time parameterization exposes instantaneous velocity, acceleration, and jerk to dynamic validation, and the independent safety permission path must verify that motor windings can generate the required torques under dynamic load and that sufficient clearance exists along the arm’s swept stopping volume before any motion is admitted.

↪ The runtime gatekeeper that checks every admitted setpoint against live state is developed in The Per-Tick Gatekeeper.

2 \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) continuity at the motor: In permanent-magnet synchronous motors, electromagnetic torque couples directly to quadrature stator current (\(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\)). Under this model an acceleration step requires a finite step in torque and current, while a velocity step implies an ideal acceleration impulse, which no drive can supply.

A multi-step action chunk delivers a finite horizon of future setpoints that the low-level tracking controller consumes sequentially while the proposer computes the next chunk. Splicing successive chunks introduces an execution seam: the temporal and kinematic boundary where the retiring setpoints hand off control to the incoming plan. Matching position alone across this seam leaves a discontinuous velocity step, and matching velocity while neglecting acceleration leaves an instantaneous acceleration step. Because physical links and payloads carry inertia, discontinuous accelerations demand instantaneous steps in motor torque, exciting mechanical vibrations and risking overcurrent faults. The trajectory generator must therefore enforce continuity through position, velocity, and acceleration (the \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) continuity condition) while verifying that dynamic torques and swept clearance throughout the transition segment remain within safe physical envelopes.2

Decoupling the execution rate from the inference rate allows the low-level tracking controller to service motor loops on a rigid real-time clock while neural inference runs asynchronously. Setpoint buffering absorbs latency jitter, but buffered motion cannot remain valid indefinitely. Sensor observations age, dynamic obstacles shift, and localization drift accumulates over time. Consequently, the planner’s replacement deadline must account for the worst-case inference tail, guaranteeing sufficient lead time to commit to a feasible, controlled stop before either the buffered trajectory drains or the supporting environmental evidence expires.

Every admitted proposal must therefore arrive as a self-contained bundle: the active trajectory segment continuous through acceleration (\(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\)), its explicit validity deadlines, and a pre-verified stopping suffix whose required deceleration distance fits within verified obstacle clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)). An isolated safety microcontroller (MCU) inspects and validates this bundle across the proposal boundary before arming actuator outputs, caching the emergency stopping continuation in local real-time memory so it can be autonomously dispatched if communications falter.

The machine meets these obligations in a fixed order. What the planner hands to the tracking controller and the permission path comes first, together with the map from tool coordinates to the joint limits the drives enforce. Replacement timing is then sized against the inference tail, a timed candidate and its seams are checked against what the actuators can follow, and the jerk-limited stop that every admitted plan must carry is sized. The generators that propose candidates, from sampling-based search to learned action chunks, can be compared only after those obligations are fixed.

11.2 The Planning Handoff and the Trajectory Record

If the planner hands over raw joint setpoints for the reach toward the mug without binding them to the obstacle geometry, the active intent lease, and a pre-checked stop, the arm can chase an expired target or coast past its clearance the moment the next inference cycle stalls. To prevent that failure, the planner consumes the belief record, which supplies obstacle geometry and clearance. It also consumes the intent lease, which supplies the grounded goal, task tolerances, effort ceilings, and the expiry time that bounds the lease (\(\IntentLeaseExpiry\)). Throughout, the planner must respect the actuator and transmission limits of the physical body. The handoff connects those inputs to the checks that permit execution, and two things must be fixed before any replacement timing can be budgeted. The first is the map between the tool coordinates in which clearance is measured and the joint coordinates whose limits the drives enforce. The second is the record that carries a candidate across the proposal boundary, with the deadlines and the stop that bound its authority.

↩︎ The belief record and the state invariants it carries are established in The Spatial Memory Handoff and State Invariants.

11.2.1 Mapping tool motion to joint limits

A path that keeps the tool inside its speed limit and clear of obstacles in workspace can still command a joint near a straightened elbow to spin faster than its drive allows, tripping a velocity fault mid-reach. Catching that mismatch requires translating workspace motion into the manipulator’s joint coordinates. A configuration \((\JointPosition_1, \dots, \JointPosition_n)\) collects the joint positions into a single point in the configuration space3 \(\ConfigurationSpace\), and removing configurations occupied by obstacles leaves the collision-free subset \(\FreeConfigurationSpace = \ConfigurationSpace \setminus \ObstacleConfigurationSet\) (Lozano-Pérez 1983). Because a geometric path through \(\FreeConfigurationSpace\) still lacks timing, an executable trajectory assigns physical timestamps to the path, generating continuous state references \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) = [\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)^\top, \GeneralizedVelocities(\TimeInstant)^\top]^\top\) and acceleration profiles \(\GeneralizedAccelerations(\TimeInstant)\) alongside feedforward torque profiles \(\FeedforwardJointTorque(\TimeInstant)\). Whereas the kinematic references state the intended position, velocity, and acceleration, the feedforward terms supply the torque predicted by the rigid-body model. The complete candidate crosses the proposal boundary for independent checking, and only admitted actuator commands cross the causal boundary to the drives.

3 Configuration space: Lozano-Pérez (1983) shrank the moving body to a point by growing each obstacle by the body’s shape, and in the same year Schwartz and Sharir (1983) treated the “piano movers’ problem” as a search of exactly this space. The reduction turns a collision query into a membership test on \(\ObstacleConfigurationSet\), but the space gains a dimension with every joint, which is why collision queries dominate the run time of the sampling planners in section 11.6.

Lozano-Pérez, Tomás. 1983. “Spatial Planning: A Configuration Space Approach.” IEEE Transactions on Computers C-32 (2): 108–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/TC.1983.1676196.
Schwartz, Jacob T., and Micha Sharir. 1983. “On the ‘Piano Movers’ Problem. II. General Techniques for Computing Topological Properties of Real Algebraic Manifolds.” Advances in Applied Mathematics 4 (3): 298–351. https://doi.org/10.1016/0196-8858(83)90014-3.

The drives track joint angles, but clearance is measured in the task space where the tool, the mug, and the conveyor guard sit. In a planar two-link abstraction of the arm’s shoulder, elbow, and wrist with upper-arm length \(\LinkLength_1\) and forearm length \(\LinkLength_2\), forward kinematics gives the tool position directly as a function of the joint angles:

\[\ToolPlanarAbscissa = \LinkLength_1\cos \JointPosition_1 + \LinkLength_2\cos(\JointPosition_1+\JointPosition_2), \qquad \ToolPlanarOrdinate = \LinkLength_1\sin \JointPosition_1 + \LinkLength_2\sin(\JointPosition_1+\JointPosition_2) \tag{1}\]

Differentiating equation 1 gives tool velocity \(\EndEffectorVelocity = \ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\,\GeneralizedVelocities\), where the manipulator Jacobian4 \(\ManipulatorJacobian = \partial\CartesianPosition/\partial\GeneralizedCoordinates\) changes with configuration. It is the same map that showed, in Kinematic topology mismatches and null-space projection, why a Cartesian action label can force a different arm through a singularity; here it prices the arm’s own reach. For the two-link arm its determinant is \(\LinkLength_1 \LinkLength_2 \sin \JointPosition_2\), which vanishes when the elbow straightens, the singular pose at which no joint motion moves the tool along the arm.

4 Manipulator Jacobian: It is the same object automatic differentiation builds, the matrix of first derivatives of an output with respect to its inputs, here tool position with respect to joint angles. Reverse-mode autograd never forms the matrix; it computes products \(\ManipulatorJacobian^\top\UpstreamGradientVector\), and the manipulator equation uses that same transpose product to map a tool wrench into joint torques (equation 8). A planner is the unusual consumer that must invert \(\ManipulatorJacobian\), and it is the inverse, not the Jacobian, that fails at a singularity.

Consider the link lengths from the arm’s published kinematic chain: 0.316 m and 0.384 m, which yield a 0.70 m planar reach (Franka Robotics GmbH 2023). The arm’s weakest direction is the tool direction that a unit of joint rate moves least, set by the smallest singular value \(\SmallestJacobianSingularNumber\) of \(\ManipulatorJacobian\), so that \(\|\GeneralizedVelocities\| = \ToolSpeedLimit/\SmallestJacobianSingularNumber\). Moving the tool in that direction at the 1 m/s tool center point (TCP) limit requires a joint-rate vector of magnitude 4.9 rad/s at a 90° elbow. With two joints, the larger component of a vector is at least its norm divided by \(\sqrt{2}\), so at least one joint must then turn at 3.5 rad/s, above the published 2.175 rad/s limit of the shoulder and elbow joints. Straightening the arm makes the demand far worse. The image of a unit joint-rate circle under \(\ManipulatorJacobian\), the manipulability ellipse of tool velocities, is broad at the 90° elbow and collapses into a narrow sliver across the links at 10° (figure 1). Sustaining 1 m/s along the arm, the ellipse’s minor axis, then needs a joint-rate magnitude of 37.8 rad/s. A tool-speed limit bounds no joint speed near a singularity. Every tool-space quantity this chapter checks (the TCP limit, the stopping distance, and the swept clearance) must therefore be mapped back through \(\ManipulatorJacobian\) to the joint limits the drives enforce. This holds even on a seven-joint arm, whose extra degrees of freedom help the planner steer around singular poses that this planar abstraction omits.

↪ Forward kinematics, the manipulator Jacobian, singularities, and the damped least-squares inverse are developed for \(\JointCount\) joints in Forward kinematics and singularities.

The same map must also run in reverse. A proposal stated in tool space, whether a grasp pose from the intent lease or a Cartesian velocity from a policy or a teleoperation device, leaves the planner to find the joint motion that produces it, the inverse kinematics problem. The two-link arm has a closed-form inverse. A seven-joint arm replanning chunk by chunk is solved by differential steps instead: linearize at the current configuration and solve \(\ManipulatorJacobian\,\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates = \FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\) for the joint step that moves the tool by \(\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\), then repeat from the new configuration. Where \(\ManipulatorJacobian\) is invertible the exact step is \(\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates = \ManipulatorJacobian^{-1}\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\). Written along the singular directions of \(\ManipulatorJacobian\), this step divides the part of \(\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\) along each tool direction by that direction’s singular value \(\JacobianSingularNumber\). The weakest direction therefore receives the largest joint step, \(1/\SmallestJacobianSingularNumber\) per unit of tool motion, which is exactly the joint-rate demand that figure 1 charts diverging along the minor axis as the elbow straightens. An exact inverse passes the singularity’s arithmetic straight to the drives.

Damped least squares gives up exactness to bound the step (Wampler 1986; Nakamura and Hanafusa 1986). Instead of demanding \(\ManipulatorJacobian\,\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates = \FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\), it chooses the joint step that best trades tool error against joint motion, with a damping length \(\InverseKinematicsDamping\) that sets the exchange rate:

Wampler, Charles W., II. 1986. “Manipulator Inverse Kinematic Solutions Based on Vector Formulations and Damped Least-Squares Methods.” IEEE Transactions on Systems, Man, and Cybernetics 16 (1): 93–101. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSMC.1986.289285.
Nakamura, Yoshihiko, and Hideo Hanafusa. 1986. “Inverse Kinematic Solutions with Singularity Robustness for Robot Manipulator Control.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 108 (3): 163–71. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.3143764.

\[\FiniteChange\DampedStepConfiguration = \arg\min_{\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates} \left(\|\ManipulatorJacobian\,\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates-\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\|^2 + \InverseKinematicsDamping^2\|\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates\|^2\right) = \ManipulatorJacobian^\top\!\left(\ManipulatorJacobian\ManipulatorJacobian^\top+\InverseKinematicsDamping^2\IdentityMatrix\right)^{-1}\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition \tag{2}\]

Setting the gradient of the objective to zero gives \((\ManipulatorJacobian^\top\ManipulatorJacobian+\InverseKinematicsDamping^2\IdentityMatrix)\,\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates = \ManipulatorJacobian^\top\FiniteChange\CartesianPosition\), and moving \(\ManipulatorJacobian^\top\) through the inverse yields the form in equation 2, which needs only a \(2 \times 2\) solve for the planar arm. A reader who has trained a model will recognize ridge regression, with \(\InverseKinematicsDamping^2\) as the weight-decay coefficient. Along each singular direction the gain changes from \(1/\JacobianSingularNumber\) to \(\JacobianSingularNumber/(\JacobianSingularNumber^2+\InverseKinematicsDamping^2)\). Far from the singularity, where \(\JacobianSingularNumber \gg \InverseKinematicsDamping\), the two agree. As \(\JacobianSingularNumber\) falls toward zero the damped gain falls with it instead of diverging, and it never exceeds \(1/(2\InverseKinematicsDamping)\), reached where \(\JacobianSingularNumber = \InverseKinematicsDamping\). The damping thus places a configuration-independent ceiling on the joint step, which no choice of pose can breach.

With an illustrative \(\InverseKinematicsDamping\) of 50 mm, the weakest-direction request at the 1 m/s TCP limit needs 4.6 rad/s of joint rate at the 90° elbow, against 4.9 rad/s undamped, and the tool still realizes 94.3 percent of the requested motion. At the 10° elbow the damped command is 8.3 rad/s instead of 37.8 rad/s, and no pose can demand more than 10 rad/s. The price is paid where the damping acts: the tool realizes only 21.9 percent of the requested motion along the arm, so the step lags its target in exactly the direction the arm cannot move well. That residual is a tracking error the planner must carry into the trajectory’s timing and clearance checks rather than discard. A larger \(\InverseKinematicsDamping\) lowers the ceiling and enlarges the error everywhere, which is why practical solvers raise the damping only as \(\SmallestJacobianSingularNumber\) approaches it.

As the elbow straightens, the exact inverse climbs from 4.9 rad/s to 37.8 rad/s; damping caps it at 10 rad/s.

Damping bounds the inverse-kinematics step; it does not make the step feasible. Even the damped 8.3 rad/s exceeds the 2.175 rad/s joint limit, so the planner must still slow the tool through time parameterization (section 11.4.5), and the permission path still checks every joint rate against the limits the drives enforce. Damped least squares is a better proposal, not a permission.

11.2.2 The trajectory record and its deadlines

If a candidate trajectory crosses from the planner to the controller without an immutable expiration time and a pre-checked stop, a hung host process leaves the drives tracking the last command into an obstacle. What the planner hands over is therefore a trajectory record, the ninth record in the record chain and the immutable proposal that the tracking controller follows and the permission path admits. The trajectory record consumes the geometric obstacle bounds from the belief record and the task target and lease window from the intent lease. It binds an untrusted sequence proposal to checked physical limits across the formal boundary between the unprivileged, asynchronous Brain and the deterministic, hard real-time Nervous System. Its header names its coordinate frame \(\TrajectoryRecordFrame\), evidence time \(\EvidenceEpoch\), initial reference state, and active intent lease expiry \(\IntentLeaseExpiry\). The header also carries four deadlines defined in the MCU monotonic clock domain (into which host and sensor timestamps are converted with a bounded synchronization error). Nominal tracking begins at \(\TrackingStartInstant\), the nominal setpoints end at \(\NominalPlanExpiry\), the matched stop must physically begin at \(\CommitmentInstant\), and the admitted stop finishes at \(\StopCompletionInstant\). Beside these header fields, an assumption sidecar declares the payload, friction, obstacle-map epoch, and other conditions under which the candidate was checked (section 11.8). Within the planner itself, each replacement is scheduled against an earlier internal milestone, the blend deadline \(\BlendDeadline\), by which a preferred successor should clear validation. The latest replacement-admission decision on the MCU precedes physical stop onset at \(\CommitmentInstant\) by the reserved decision, transport, and actuator delay.

The permission path permits nominal TRACK only while the intent lease and trajectory record remain valid, so an expired intent lease terminates nominal tracking immediately, regardless of what the trajectory’s own timestamps allow. To manage asynchronous inference latency tails and hardware faults without compromising safety, the architecture enforces a strict two-tier deadline hierarchy:

  1. Fast Nervous System periodic chunk lease (\(\ChunkLease\), 60 ms): This high-rate heartbeat governs the real-time execution contract between Brain and Nervous System. The chunk policy proposes base velocity and arm joint targets together, so one chunk lease governs both bodies, and only an admitted replacement chunk renews it, with a desired admission interval of 50 ms on this machine. Qualification must bound successful-admission interarrival, not merely inference or request cadence. If an upstream inference pass hangs or communication drops, the lease lapses 10 ms after its expected renewal, immediately requesting a controlled stop whose onset follows the reserved response delay.
  2. Trajectory commitment deadline: The record names the latest physical onset of its matched stop. Replacement admission must close early enough to dispatch that stop and absorb actuator response delay. This geometric limit does not replace the running machine’s shorter chunk lease; the later worked timeline examines an explicitly different, longer-lease configuration.

↩︎ The chunk lease and its sizing from the stopping budget are established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget.

Two stopping responses back these deadlines. A matched stop suffix, latched at admission, begins at \(\CommitmentInstant\) if no successor has been admitted; a state-matched fallback, built from the measured state, answers any earlier invalidation, such as a lapsed lease (section 11.5.1). Either stop remains authorized after nominal tracking expires, provided its own state, clearance, sensing, and drive-energy premises still hold.

Definition 11.1: Stopping suffix

Stopping suffix is the bounded continuation, checked and latched with a trajectory at admission, that brings the machine from a declared entry state to rest along the already-checked path, within the clearance that remains and without further input from the proposer.

  1. Significance: It is the term that makes \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) checkable before motion begins. From the arm’s 1 m/s TCP limit, the worked suffix sized in section 11.5.2 travels 137.5 mm of an illustrative 300 mm of clearance, the free distance along the checked path between the tool and the nearest obstacle. The stop alone therefore claims nearly half the room before any response delay is charged.
  2. Distinction: The matched suffix is anchored at the nominal state expected at \(\CommitmentInstant\); the state-matched fallback is built from the measured state when authority ends earlier. Both are controlled stops along the checked path, unlike a drive inhibit or brake, whose motion is not planned.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the last nominal setpoint, or a hold at the final position, as the stop. A hold is not a deceleration profile, and a suffix sized with constant deceleration understates the distance any drive that ramps its deceleration will travel.

The two execution consumers read distinct subsets of the trajectory record. The downstream tracking controller interpolates admitted setpoints, timestamps, and feedforward torque terms to drive the motor inverters along a continuous profile fetched from the real-time execution jitter buffer. Simultaneously, the independent permission path inspects the record’s lineage, timing bounds, dynamic assumptions, validity envelopes, and verified stopping continuation (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)). The permission path monitors current limits and physical tracking error on every millisecond tick without inheriting the planner’s feasibility claim as proof. An unmodeled obstacle, payload slip, or contact wrench immediately revokes TRACK authority and activates the state-matched fallback. Because every contract field maps to an explicit hardware or software gate, the Nervous System safely executes fast physical motion without presuming that subsequent neural inference cycles will complete on schedule. A record of this kind is short-lived by design, so the next constraint is how often a successor must arrive and how long producing one takes.

11.3 Replacement Timing and the Planning Horizon

On the mug reach, the arm needs an admitted replacement every 50 ms and loses tracking authority 60 ms after the last one. Whether a replacement can arrive that often depends on how long it takes to produce one, and producing one is more than a single inference pass. The observation behind the candidate must be captured, the proposer must run, and the candidate must cross to the safety MCU and pass validation before it is admitted. Every generator, from numerical trajectory optimization to an autoregressive policy, pays for that pipeline. The timing questions therefore come before the choice of generator: how long a replacement takes, how deep into its latency tail the design must look, how much motion one record must cover, and from which state the next candidate must be planned.

11.3.1 Action chunk horizons and reaction lag

If a replacement chunk takes longer from camera capture to MCU admission than the active chunk’s lease allows, the arm drops into its stopping fallback before the new plan arrives. If the executed prefix is stretched to hide that latency, the arm reacts too slowly when the mug or an obstacle shifts. Sizing the prediction horizon \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) therefore starts from an end-to-end latency budget for replacement, one that spans host accelerator memory, the inter-processor link, and real-time microcontroller firmware.

The scheduling metric that governs every handoff is replacement latency \(\ReplacementLatency\): the total elapsed wall-clock time from the initial physical capture of the sensor observation on which a candidate trajectory proposal rests to the formal admission of that proposal into the real-time execution queue. Formulated in the event taxonomy of The capture-to-brake-onset timing budget of the running machine, \(\ReplacementLatency\) spans the interval from capture to admission. It is the direct sum of two intervals. The first is the capture-to-dispatch perception age \(\DispatchObservationAge\) established in The latency waterfall. The second is the dispatch-to-admission interval \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), a design allocation whose stages are listed next and which The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval later tests against the hardware: \[\ReplacementLatency = \underbrace{\DispatchObservationAge}_{\text{capture}\,\to\,\text{dispatch}} + \underbrace{\DispatchAdmissionInterval}_{\text{dispatch}\,\to\,\text{admission}}, \qquad \DispatchAdmissionInterval = \ProposerInferenceTime + \ProposalLinkTransportTime + \ProposalDeserializationTime + \CombinedPlanValidationAllowance + \ActivationStagingTime + \PermissionTickWaitTime \tag{3}\] On the physical machine, sensor data is already 51.6 ms old when planning dispatches. The subsequent dispatch-to-admission allocation \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) of 63.3 ms accounts for the chunk policy’s full 40 ms neural network forward pass and 18 ms of whole-plan kinematic validation. The balance is a few milliseconds of inter-board link transport, message deserialization, activation staging, and synchronization wait until the next discrete permission tick. The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval itemizes this balance when it tests the allocation on the selected hardware. These stages combine to yield an overall replacement latency \(\ReplacementLatency\) of 114.9 ms. \(\ReplacementLatency\) is a latency, not a period. It exceeds both the 50 ms renewal period and the 60 ms lease, so the renewal cadence can be sustained only by overlapping requests, several in flight at once, a throughput condition the timeline below returns to. Charging the entire neural inference pass after dispatch intentionally bills the visual encoding stage twice, since that stage is already inside \(\DispatchObservationAge\), which makes the budget deliberately conservative. When pipelined stages execute concurrently or contend for shared memory buses, the sum of independent stage percentiles does not equal the percentile of the sum (The end-to-end sense-to-actuation latency pipeline). Sizing the planning horizon therefore demands evaluating the empirical end-to-end distribution of \(\ReplacementLatency\), measured directly between physical sensor capture interrupts and microcontroller admission timestamps.

A 114.9 ms replacement path outlasts the 50 ms renewal, so requests must overlap.

Trajectory validation represents the motion planner’s dedicated compute contribution within this latency budget. The 18 ms validation allowance covers the combined elapsed contribution of host geometry validation and bounded local MCU admission, including scheduling delays, rather than overhead silently absorbed into the millisecond actuator tick. Its split between processors remains to be qualified. Formal admission requires bounded staging memory, candidate spline and stopping coefficients, and qualified local clearance evidence; the host processor’s full, multi-gigabyte scene representation need not reside on the embedded MCU. The real-time scheduler must preserve deterministic per-tick enforcement while background admission executes, refreshing the seam boundary check immediately prior to commitment. System architects must allocate and qualify this compute and memory footprint under worst-case shared-bus interference. Because each term in equation 3 represents an illustrative design allocation, neither \(\ReplacementLatency\) nor its constituent stages serve as unverified static bounds; both intervals remain explicit qualification obligations on the physical hardware.

Prediction length and execution duration are separate architectural parameters. While the prediction horizon \(\ActionChunkHorizon\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) specifies how far into the future the sequence proposer forecasts, the executed prefix represents only the initial slice dispatched to actuators before an admitted replacement plan supersedes it. Expanding the prediction horizon supplies candidate continuity well past the next planned handoff, but does not grant the robot authority to execute that trajectory unchecked. The production controller targets an admitted renewal every 50 ms, enforcing hard lease expiry at 60 ms following the most recent admission. The evidence horizon of the upstream target observation can be shorter still.

Compute amortization depends strictly on inference dispatch frequency rather than the raw number of future setpoints generated in a forward pass. Executing a longer trajectory prefix lowers invocation frequency and amortizes accelerator workload, yet it simultaneously inflates closed-loop reaction lag, delaying task corrections when the environment drifts. Conversely, predicting farther into the future while maintaining a high renewal rate increases proposer compute without reducing inference duty. Emergency protective stopping remains governed by an independent hardware permission path and its certified reaction latency. Sizing the prediction horizon therefore balances closed-loop reactivity, pipeline renewal throughput, sensor evidence freshness, and fallback transition rates under realistic system loads; no universal optimal horizon emerges from trajectory buffer capacity alone.

↩︎ Action chunking as a supply lever is established in Action chunking.

11.3.2 Tail latency budgeting across a mission

When the resident fallback works as certified, a late replacement costs availability rather than safety. A missed replanning deadline becomes a collision hazard only if the resident stop is kinematically infeasible, corrupted in memory, or delayed beyond its budgeted stopping distance. Over a long mission, however, the number of handoffs makes tail latency decide whether the mission completes,5 even when a stopping profile safely absorbs every individual miss. The analysis below therefore bounds how often handoffs miss their deadlines, not how often the machine collides.

5 Tail at scale: Dean and Barroso (2013) showed that a distributed request fanned out across many servers is bottlenecked by the slowest tail response, transforming rare single-server delays into frequent user-facing latencies. A continuous mission comprising thousands of sequential handoffs maps this same fan-out onto the time domain. Because every individual handoff must meet its admission deadline to avoid triggering a stop, the complete mission inherits the worst-case tail latency, rendering conventional per-handoff p99 benchmarks insufficient for assessing end-to-end mission reliability.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80.

The component breakdown in equation 3 defines the latency of a single replacement cycle. Because an autonomous mission executes this handoff cycle repeatedly, the tail of the latency distribution dictates system behavior. A mission requiring \(\MissionHandoffCount\) successive plan handoffs fails its continuous execution target if even one handoff misses its deadline. Partitioning a cumulative mission exceedance budget \(\MissionExceedanceBudget\) uniformly across all \(\MissionHandoffCount\) handoffs yields an upper bound on allowable per-handoff failure probability via the union bound:

\[\HandoffExceedanceProbability \le \frac{\MissionExceedanceBudget}{\MissionHandoffCount} \tag{4}\]

Napkin Math 11.1: Handoff deadlines across a mission
Problem: A generic replanner, slower than this machine’s chunk renewal, meets a p99 replacement budget on each handoff. Over a ten-minute mission, how likely is it that every handoff arrives on time, and what per-handoff tail must the design target instead?

Variables:

  • Mission length 600 s and replanning rate 5 Hz (illustrative)
  • Per-handoff exceedance of the p99 budget, \(\LateHandoffProbability=0.01\) (definitional)
  • Mission exceedance target \(\MissionExceedanceBudget=1 \times 10^{-3}\) (chosen)
  • Independent misses (assumption)

Math:

  1. Handoff count: 600 s \(\times\) 5 Hz gives \(\MissionHandoffCount=3000\), and \(\MissionHandoffCount\LateHandoffProbability=3000\times0.01=30\) handoffs are expected late.
  2. Every handoff on time: \((0.99)^{3000}\approx8 \times 10^{-14}\).
  3. Design tail (equation 4): \(\HandoffExceedanceProbability\le\MissionExceedanceBudget/\MissionHandoffCount\approx3.3 \times 10^{-7}\), the \(\text{p99.99997}\) quantile, deeper than the p99 budget by \(0.01/(3.3 \times 10^{-7})\approx3 \times 10^{4}\).

Result: A mission almost never completes with every handoff inside a p99 budget, and the tail a mission target implies lies more than four orders of magnitude deeper.

Systems insight: The quantile a design must target is set by how many handoffs a mission makes, not by the per-handoff budget, and it lies beyond what a test campaign of practical size observes. A late handoff must degrade service availability, not safety clearance. Preserving \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) relies on the resident stop profile (the pre-validated stopping suffix latched in MCU memory at admission and triggered without the host), not on optimistic assumptions about latency tails.

This per-handoff quantile functions as a design quantile target, a risk allocation across the software stack rather than an observed tail latency. In practice, resource contention across shared memory, PCIe buses, and hardware accelerators introduces temporal correlation, clustering deadline misses and violating independent-failure assumptions. Hardware-in-the-loop stress testing under maximum bus and memory saturation can expose deadline misses, but establishing this deep quantile requires a stated statistical model, sufficient observations, and evidence that the operating conditions match. The mission architecture must tolerate fallback frequencies that the available evidence cannot rule out without requiring human intervention.

Spread over 3000 handoffs, a mission target demands a \(\text{p99.99997}\) deadline, far past p99.

To maintain continuous tracking, the client dispatches the next planning request at an offset no later than \(\ReplanRequestOffset\) after the active chunk begins executing. Plan admission must complete before a hard decision cutoff that precedes physical commitment \(\CommitmentInstant\) by the actuator response allowance, rather than running up against the final nominal setpoint. Given a replacement-latency quantile target \(\DesignLatencyQuantile(\ReplacementLatency)\) and a safety reserve \(\ReplacementTimingReserve\), a sufficient conservative timing requirement is \(\CommitmentInstant-\TrackingStartInstant\ge \ReplanRequestOffset+\DesignLatencyQuantile(\ReplacementLatency)+\ReplacementTimingReserve\). This requirement charges the complete capture-to-admission quantile after request dispatch, conservatively charging the preceding capture-to-dispatch age again. That extra age charge is separate from the visual-encoding overlap in equation 3. A tighter dispatch-relative requirement would use an independently characterized dispatch-to-admission distribution; subtracting marginal latency quantiles would not establish it. The reserve parameter \(\ReplacementTimingReserve\) includes both scheduling slack before preferred admission and the physical decision-to-onset allowance. Furthermore, executing the resident stop requires an additional time window \(\StoppingDuration\) under dedicated MCU authority. Consequently, the time horizon \(\TrajectoryRecordDuration\) that an admitted trajectory record must cover, whether stored as dense setpoint arrays or polynomial splines, satisfies the corresponding conservative design requirement:

\[\TrajectoryRecordDuration\ge \ReplanRequestOffset+\DesignLatencyQuantile(\ReplacementLatency)+\ReplacementTimingReserve+\StoppingDuration \tag{5}\]

In the baseline warehouse configuration, the chunk policy outputs a discrete setpoint every 25 ms across a 16-step horizon, producing 400 ms of nominal forward trajectory. Under nominal operation, an admitted replacement supersedes this trajectory after 50 ms. If renewal fails, the active lease lapses at 60 ms past the preceding admission tick. Unexecuted setpoints beyond that cutoff serve as candidate references for continuous handoffs rather than extended tracking authority.

On this running schedule the second tier, the record’s commitment deadline, never acts, because a stalled proposer lets the 60 ms chunk lease lapse long before commitment arrives. To show how a record’s own deadlines coordinate nominal motion with its stop, the worked timeline therefore uses an alternate longer-lease schedule for the same arm, one whose chunk lease is long enough not to lapse before the commitment cutoff. The commitment tier can then be seen on its own. On this schedule the planner issues its request 60 ms after the active chunk begins (later than the running machine’s 50 ms renewal, which only a longer lease permits). It budgets 160 ms as its design-tail replacement latency \(\DesignLatencyQuantile(\ReplacementLatency)\) above the 114.9 ms nominal sum, as a chosen stand-in for the deep quantile that equation 4 implies. It then adds 20 ms of scheduling slack to set a preferred admission deadline at 240 ms. Adding the 22 ms decision-to-onset response allowance to the scheduling slack gives the 42 ms full reserve of equation 5 and a conservative minimum stop onset of 262 ms. Selecting a slightly later physical stopping onset at 300 ms leaves 60 ms between preferred admission and commitment. Replacement admission can therefore close as late as 278 ms (one 22 ms decision-to-onset delay before commitment). The pre-validated 275 ms stopping suffix (section 11.5.2) then brings the arm to rest at 575 ms, provided clearance holds through that entire window.

This alternate profile serves to contrast buffer structure with the production policy. Its window spans 23 setpoint periods, yet it stays compact in memory because the stop is stored as an analytical profile (jerk limits, acceleration limits, and phase durations) rather than as a dense array of setpoints. Set on one time axis, the two schedules spend clearance differently (figure 2). On the running lease, the lease lapses at 60 ms, the tick, bus, and onset allowance delays the resident stop to 82 ms, and the arm uses 219.5 mm of the 300 mm clearance, leaving 80.5 mm. On the alternate schedule, admission must close by 278 ms, after the preferred 240 ms, so that the matched stop begins at the 300 ms commitment and reaches rest at 575 ms with 102.5 mm to spare. The nominal setpoints that run on to 400 ms are never executed.

Sustaining the continuous 50 ms renewal cadence of the running lease requires verifying pipeline throughput alongside latency. Because the illustrative 114.9 ms replacement latency exceeds the 50 ms renewal period, the system must pipeline consecutive planning requests. Pipelining maintains real-time throughput only when intermediate hardware stages provide non-blocking queue capacity and bounded bus arbitration delay; pipelined concurrency cannot be established from latency sums alone. Until comprehensive load testing validates multi-request pipelining under peak bus contention, the specified resident fallback responds to missed renewal only under its stated state, clearance, sensing, energy, drive-response, deceleration, and jerk assumptions.

Both lease architectures remain bounded by the validity lifespan of upstream perceptual evidence. Even when employing a qualified local object tracker, the tracked evidence horizon derived in Expiring Goals and Temporal Validity Leases is shorter than the alternate commitment duration, so a single vision observation of a target object cannot justify that entire duration. Instead, the manipulator uses a staged perceptual approach. It follows an admitted prefix through a certified clear corridor, triggers fresh visual acquisition from a wrist-mounted camera at closer proximity, and re-anchors subsequent trajectory segments to updated state estimates. Embedded firmware then admits the terminal grasp maneuver only when the complete motion profile satisfies current target tolerances within an active validity envelope. Although an overall reaching motion may span several observation cycles, every executed trajectory slice requires fresh evidentiary support. Generating a new trajectory proposal cannot refresh stale sensor evidence, and losing perceptual tracking immediately transitions the robot into a state-matched stopping fallback.

On the Push-T manipulation benchmark (Chi et al. 2025), frequent closed-loop replanning lets an articulated planar arm recover from live disturbances as it guides a T-shaped block toward a designated pose. A diffusion policy generates \(\ActionChunkHorizon = 16\) future steps and executes an initial prefix of \(\ExecutedActionStepCount = 6\) steps before replanning. It recovers dynamically from transient camera occlusions, external block displacements during approach, and perturbations near the terminal pose. However, these empirical trials demonstrate task-level reactive correction rather than certified derivative continuity or deterministic stopping under compute starvation. A safety-critical production system still requires an independently executable stopping suffix verified against known kinematic clearance bounds.

11.3.3 Conditioning on the state at splice time

While a joint rotates at 0.50 rad/s during an illustrative 80 ms compute and transport delay (somewhat longer than the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation), a joint pose sampled when the replacement request was dispatched lags the advancing reference by 0.040 rad (2.29°). Mapped through the arm’s forward kinematics (equation 1) at its 0.70 m maximum planar reach, that angular lag displaces the tool center point by up to 28 mm. This is more than the entire lateral swept-volume clearance budgeted for a reach past the conveyor guard (section 11.7.2). That clearance is a sideways allowance, distinct from the 300 mm of free path ahead of the tool that a stop consumes. Conditioning a motion plan on sensor feedback captured when inference begins therefore induces the stale-state paradox. By the time the generative policy completes its forward pass, the physical arm has already advanced along its prior trajectory. Initializing the new plan from the request-time state then commands the actuators to step backward across the distance traversed during computation, which injects step discontinuities into commanded position, velocity, and acceleration. To eliminate boundary discontinuities, the sequence proposer must instead condition its action generation on the forward-projected reference state6 of the active trajectory at the designated replanning seam \(\SeamInstant\):

6 Smith predictor: Smith (1957) handled known dead time in feedback control loops by projecting a plant model forward across the delay interval rather than closing feedback against delayed measurements. Future-state conditioning adapts this idea to trajectory planning by anchoring prospective trajectories to the reference state expected at the handoff seam. It also inherits the weakness of any dead-time predictor: external disturbances during the delay interval go uncompensated until physical feedback arrives, which is why the MCU must check measured joint states against strict error bounds at activation.

Smith, Otto J. M. 1957. “Closer Control of Loops with Dead Time.” Chemical Engineering Progress 53 (5): 217–19.

\[\FiniteChange \ReferenceJointPosition=\int_{t_0}^{\SeamInstant}\RetiringChunkJointVelocity(\TimeInstant)\,d\TimeInstant,\qquad \ProjectedSeamState=\RetiringChunkReferenceState(\SeamInstant) \tag{6}\]

The left-hand integral is the joint-space gap that a stale anchor would leave, the 0.040 rad of the example, while the right-hand state is the anchor the proposer uses instead. Because the projected seam state \(\ProjectedSeamState\) in equation 6 exists strictly in reference trajectory space, external contact forces, mechanical compliance, and closed-loop tracking errors invariably displace the physical plant from this nominal point. Rather than presuming an unobservable future measurement, admission verification prior to the seam evaluates a bounded reachable-state envelope, confirming that feasible polynomial bridges and certified stopping profiles exist across all reachable states within that set. At activation, embedded MCU firmware reads fresh optical encoder measurements and state-estimator error bounds to validate the actual boundary discrepancy \((\FiniteChange \JointPosition, \FiniteChange\JointVelocity, \FiniteChange\JointAcceleration)\) against that certified envelope. It then instantiates the bounded fifth-degree \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) bridge of section 11.4.4 and checks its joint-space derivatives against actuator velocity, torque, and jerk limits within the reserved real-time tick before dispatching motor commands. If measured tracking errors or bridge derivatives exceed admitted bounds, the MCU rejects the replacement and maintains the active plan only while its tracking error, target evidence, lease duration, and stopping clearances satisfy safety invariants; otherwise, it triggers the pre-latched fallback profile. Matching two reference profiles guarantees derivative continuity across reference commands, but it never eliminates physical tracking errors in the underlying mechanical hardware.

Recent generative imitation learning architectures incorporate delay compensation directly into their sampling algorithms. Real-time chunking (Black et al. 2025) computes upcoming action sequences concurrently with active chunk execution. Because setpoints scheduled to run during the inference delay window are already committed to the actuator queue, the model fixes those initial steps to their committed values and samples subsequent actions conditioned on this prefix, applying decaying consistency weights across the overlap window. Although empirical evaluations show that control throughput remains stable under artificial latency injection, the formulation provides no formal continuity or stability guarantees. In contrast, bidirectional decoding (Liu et al. 2024) samples a diverse candidate batch of trajectory chunks, scoring and selecting the proposal whose trajectory matches the previously executed actions most smoothly. Conditioning generation on the committed trajectory prefix promotes smooth kinematic transitions and reduces boundary tracking discrepancies, yet it cannot substitute for formal safety verification. Embedded microcontroller logic must still evaluate actual physical states against the admitted safety envelope before committing any new chunk to execution.

Liu, Yuejiang, Jubayer Ibn Hamid, Annie Xie, Yoonho Lee, Maximilian Du, and Chelsea Finn. 2024. “Bidirectional Decoding: Improving Action Chunking via Guided Test-Time Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2408.17355.
Definition 11.2: Future-state conditioning

Future-state conditioning is the practice of generating candidate motion plans anchored to the anticipated state at execution time rather than the stale state measured at plan initiation.

  1. Significance: Offsets computational and transport latency \(\FiniteChange \PlanningStartInstant\), eliminating boundary step discontinuities at trajectory handoffs without forcing zero-velocity pauses between consecutive chunks.
  2. Distinction: Differs from state estimation or Kalman filtering; it is a forward reference-planning technique that propagates an admitted nominal trajectory rather than filtering sensor noise.
  3. Common pitfall: Conflating forward-projected reference states with actual physical plant positions. If external contact forces or tracking lag displace the mechanism during planning, activation remains contingent upon the MCU verifying actual encoder feedback and bridge feasibility at the handoff seam.

Trajectory replacement therefore comes down to three sizing decisions. The prediction horizon must be long enough to carry kinematically feasible motion together with a certified stopping profile, the executed prefix short enough to keep closed-loop responses to a changing environment fast, and the renewal cadence one that the pipelined compute can sustain deterministically. Perceptual evidence freshness dictates the maximum lifespan over which any target estimate justifies physical motion. Even when replacement plans arrive strictly within latency budgets, timely delivery alone cannot guarantee that physical joint actuators can track the synthesized trajectory or bridge seamlessly from their measured state.

Checkpoint 11.1: Action chunk horizon and tail latency

Verify your understanding of replacement timing budgets, tail latency exposure, and delay compensation mechanisms before examining kinodynamic feasibility:

11.4 Kinodynamic Feasibility

A candidate trajectory that clears every obstacle in workspace and arrives before its deadline will still fault on the hardware if it asks a joint for more speed, torque, or current slew than the drive can supply. What an embodied model generates is a geometric and timed proposal, not permission to move. While high-level motion planners and learned policies navigate free configuration space \(\FreeConfigurationSpace\), physical actuators operate under fixed electromotive, thermal, and structural limits. Kinodynamic feasibility bridges geometric intent and mechanical reality, ensuring that reference trajectories respect actuator torque limits, velocity boundaries, and transmission dynamics before the low-level controller dispatches setpoints to motor drives.

Definition 11.3: Kinodynamic feasibility envelope

Kinodynamic feasibility envelope is the set of initial states from which the machine can track a candidate trajectory, including its transition seams and terminal stop, while every joint remains within its configured position, velocity, acceleration, jerk (the rate of change of acceleration), torque, and stator current limits.

  1. Significance: Obstacle clearance in configuration space is necessary but insufficient. At the 1 m/s TCP speed limit, a collision-free reach in the arm’s weakest kinematic direction at a right-angle elbow demands 3.5 rad/s from a single joint against its 2.175 rad/s ceiling. The spatial geometry succeeds while the timing fails. The envelope defines the state region where dynamic models predict trackable motion, which admission and runtime monitors must continuously verify.
  2. Distinction: Kinematic reachability evaluates whether a mechanism can geometrically achieve a configuration. Kinodynamic feasibility introduces time and momentum: the time derivatives that a trajectory profile implies, the dynamic torques produced through the manipulator equation, the effective inertia reflected through gear trains, and the physical stopping distance required to brake safely.
  3. Common pitfall: Evaluating feasibility solely at discrete waypoints. The continuous-time interpolant between discrete samples can overshoot velocity bounds or demand severe torque steps even when every discrete sample satisfies constraints.

11.4.1 Rigid-body dynamics and continuous collision checks

A trajectory can stay completely clear of obstacles in \(\FreeConfigurationSpace\) and still fail on the physical machine because it demands actuator torques that saturate motor inverters, induces base accelerations that tip the platform, or drives current slew rates that trip motor protection circuits. Classical kinematic motion planning checked only whether the swept volume avoided obstacles; a moving body with momentum must satisfy configuration-space clearance and time-domain dynamic limits together.

On a single joint, such as the manipulator’s shoulder lifting a grasped payload, the balance of torques obeys the scalar equation of motion:

\[\CommandedJointTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia\,\JointAcceleration + \Mass \Gravity \PayloadLeverArm\cos \JointPosition + \JointFrictionTorque \tag{7}\]

where \(\EffectiveJointInertia\) represents the effective inertia driven by the joint, \(\Mass\) is a concentrated mass whose center of gravity lies at distance \(\PayloadLeverArm\) from the joint axis, \(\JointPosition\) is the joint angle measured from the horizontal plane, and \(\JointFrictionTorque\) is friction torque opposing motion. Mechanical inertia resists angular acceleration, while gravity exerts a static load proportional to the horizontal moment arm \(\PayloadLeverArm\cos \JointPosition\). Holding the rated 3 kg payload horizontally at the manipulator’s full 0.70 m planar reach requires 20.6 N·m of static holding torque, which consumes 23.7 percent of the joint’s 87 N·m torque limit before motion begins (Franka Robotics GmbH 2023). The actuator’s reflected rotor inertia is the \(\GearReductionRatio^2\)-amplified rotor term of Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching, which the seam analysis below prices for a Panda-class joint. Adding it to the payload inertia produces an effective joint inertia of 1.65 kg·m² that every commanded acceleration must accelerate. Structural link mass and distributed inertia add to this baseline. Because manufacturer datasheets rarely provide link mass distributions, empirical evaluations rely on published identified parameters (Gaz et al. 2019); an operational deployment requires system identification on the physical arm and attached tooling.

Gaz, Claudio, Marco Cognetti, Alexander Oliva, Paolo Robuffo Giordano, and Alessandro De Luca. 2019. “Dynamic Identification of the Franka Emika Panda Robot with Retrieval of Feasible Parameters Using Penalty-Based Optimization.” IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters 4 (4): 4147–54. https://doi.org/10.1109/LRA.2019.2931248.
Craig, John J. 2005. Introduction to Robotics: Mechanics and Control. 3rd ed. Pearson Prentice Hall.

In multi-degree-of-freedom manipulators, dynamic coupling interconnects the joints. The scalar terms generalize into the matrix terms of the manipulator equation, derived from the Euler-Lagrange equations of motion (Craig 2005; Lynch and Park 2017):

\[\JointTorque(\TimeInstant) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) - \ManipulatorJacobian^\top(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\ExternalWrench \tag{8}\]

Here, the symmetric, positive-definite inertia matrix \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) generalizes scalar inertia \(\EffectiveJointInertia\) from equation 7. Off-diagonal elements capture cross-joint inertial coupling (accelerating the elbow induces reaction torques at the shoulder), and matrix values vary continuously as the arm articulates. The quadratic velocity term \(\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities\) aggregates centrifugal and Coriolis torques, which scale quadratically with joint velocity, while the gravity vector \(\GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) generalizes \(\Mass \Gravity \PayloadLeverArm\cos \JointPosition\) to represent configuration-dependent static holding torque. The geometric Jacobian from equation 1 maps an external wrench \(\ExternalWrench\) acting on the end effector into equivalent joint torques via the transpose \(\ManipulatorJacobian^\top(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\); external contact wrenches that assist motion reduce actuator torque demands, while resistive loads increase them. Evaluating equation 8 along a prospective trajectory \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)\) is the problem of inverse dynamics. The recursive Newton-Euler algorithm computes inverse dynamics for an \(\JointCount\)-joint serial arm in \(\mathcal{O}(\JointCount)\) arithmetic operations per trajectory sample (Luh et al. 1980; Lynch and Park 2017). An admission filter scanning an \(\TrajectorySampleCount\)-sample candidate trajectory therefore performs \(\mathcal{O}(\TrajectorySampleCount \JointCount)\) operations whose worst-case execution time must fit within the system’s admission latency allowance.

Luh, John Y. S., Michael W. Walker, and Richard P. C. Paul. 1980. “On-Line Computational Scheme for Mechanical Manipulators.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 102 (2): 69–76. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.3149599.

Before dispatching a proposed trajectory chunk, the planning runtime screens it against the kinodynamic feasibility envelope, verifying that the physical state at the handoff seam satisfies position, velocity, torque, stator current, contact force, and collision boundaries over the planning horizon. For robotic arms, the rigid-body model maps joint trajectories \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)\), \(\GeneralizedVelocities(\TimeInstant)\), and \(\GeneralizedAccelerations(\TimeInstant)\) to joint torques \(\JointTorque(\TimeInstant)\) via equation 8. Those torques convert to quadrature-axis motor currents \({\TorqueProducingCurrent}_i(\TimeInstant) = \CommandedJointTorque_i(\TimeInstant) / {\JointTorqueConstant}_i\) using the joint-effective torque constant \({\JointTorqueConstant}_i\). (When using motor-side torque constants, the conversion must explicitly incorporate gear ratios and transmission mechanical efficiencies.) Initial screening by an unprivileged planner, however, does not constitute system admission. The real-time safety MCU executes an independent, deterministic verification check, admitting a bounded trajectory chunk only if configured dynamic limits and certified stop clearances hold for the verified initial state. Once admitted, the MCU monitors control tracking error, plan freshness, and stopping distance margins on every servo cycle.

Numerical optimizers can smooth geometric paths and expand obstacle clearance, but motion permission resides strictly in the admitted trajectory chunk and its resident stopping suffix. For example, the cuRobo motion-generation framework uses massively parallel GPU compute to generate collision-free, minimum-jerk trajectories across kinematic configurations (Sundaralingam et al. 2023). Its collision pipeline evaluates signed distance fields across sampled swept-sphere volumes under a linear-motion approximation, leaving continuous inter-sample swept volume evaluation as an open requirement. Following trajectory optimization, cuRobo identifies the limiting time step where joint velocity, acceleration, or jerk encounters actuator bounds, rescaling trajectory duration before re-optimizing. This uniform temporal scaling represents an initial form of the rigorous time parameterization developed in section 11.4.5. Because cuRobo’s algorithmic formulation focuses on kinematic derivatives rather than full multi-body dynamics, torque boundaries, winding thermal accumulation, continuous swept-volume containment, and admission deadlines remain external system-level requirements. Under this systems architecture, the GPU trajectory generator operates as an unprivileged proposer, while the real-time safety MCU independently validates prospective trajectories and seam constraints within a strict admission deadline, latching an executable stopping continuation before authorizing motion.

11.4.2 Overshoot between samples and contracted limits

When the admission filter checks a candidate only at discrete waypoints spaced \(\SamplingPeriod\) apart, two consecutive joint samples \(\TimeInstant_k = \SetpointSampleIndex \SamplingPeriod\) that sit just below the velocity ceiling \(\SpeedBound\) can still bracket an overspeed fault midway between them. The reason is that physical actuators track a continuous interpolant across \((\TimeInstant_k, \TimeInstant_{k+1})\). If that interpolator can command accelerations up to \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) within a sample period, an acceleration sign reversal across the midpoint lets continuous velocity rise above the values recorded at both endpoints.

Under acceleration-bounded interpolation, the maximum velocity excursion between two consecutive samples sharing equal velocity arises from a bang-bang acceleration reversal: \(+\ProfileAccelerationBound\) applied over \([0, \SamplingPeriod/2]\), followed immediately by \(-\ProfileAccelerationBound\) over \([\SamplingPeriod/2, \SamplingPeriod]\) to restore endpoint velocity. The velocity excursion reaches its maximum at the midpoint, corresponding to the integrated area of the initial acceleration phase:

\[\FiniteChange \JointVelocity = \ProfileAccelerationBound \cdot \frac{\SamplingPeriod}{2} = \frac{1}{2} \ProfileAccelerationBound \SamplingPeriod \tag{9}\]

As equation 9 demonstrates, two consecutive waypoint samples evaluated just beneath velocity ceiling \(\SpeedBound\) can bracket an interior velocity peak that exceeds the limit, tripping drive overspeed protection between individually valid samples. To ensure continuous-time velocity compliance without evaluating an infinite continuum of points, the admission filter tests discrete waypoints against an analytically contracted velocity threshold:

\[|\JointVelocity_k| \le \SpeedBound - \frac{1}{2} \ProfileAccelerationBound \SamplingPeriod \tag{10}\]

The shaded acceleration area in figure 3 becomes the velocity rise between the sampled endpoints. Placing both endpoints one full rise below the physical ceiling keeps the continuous peak at that ceiling.

The endpoint check succeeds only when the interpolant obeys the assumptions used to reserve the margin. A sampled value below the physical ceiling alone supplies no such guarantee.

This contraction creates a safety margin that absorbs the worst-case overshoot any acceleration-bounded interpolator can generate across a sample period \(\SamplingPeriod\). A smaller reserve requires additional boundary conditions. If consecutive samples have equal velocity and zero acceleration, and the interpolator enforces \(\JerkLimit \le 4\ProfileAccelerationBound/\SamplingPeriod\), the extremal reversal consists of a positive triangular acceleration pulse followed by its negative counterpart, each of base \(\SamplingPeriod/2\). Their peaks reach \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) only at equality in the jerk condition, so \(\frac{1}{4} \ProfileAccelerationBound \SamplingPeriod\) is a conservative velocity reserve for this restricted interpolation. A jerk bound alone does not justify that reserve when endpoint accelerations are nonzero. For polynomial spline interpolants, the admission filter can instead compute exact interior extrema analytically before certifying continuous-time feasibility.

11.4.3 Seam acceleration jumps and reflected rotor inertia

When the arm hands off from one action chunk to the next while moving, two chunks that are each feasible in isolation can still form an infeasible seam between them. Matching joint positions alone (\(\DifferentiabilityClass^0\) continuity) leaves an unconstrained velocity step across the boundary. Matching both position and velocity (\(\DifferentiabilityClass^1\) continuity) eliminates velocity jumps but leaves an instantaneous acceleration discontinuity, which demands an immediate step in joint torque. Under a rigid-inertia approximation, commanding a velocity step \(\FiniteChange\JointVelocity\) across a single servo period \(\SamplingPeriod\) requires an inertial torque of:

\[\SeamStepTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia \frac{\FiniteChange\JointVelocity}{\SamplingPeriod} \tag{11}\]

where \(\EffectiveJointInertia = \LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\) in equation 11 represents the total effective inertia referenced to the joint output. This term adds link and payload inertia \(\LoadInertia\) to the reflected rotor inertia established in Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching, the motor rotor inertia \(\RotorInertia\) seen through gear reduction ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\). Because the rotor turns \(\GearReductionRatio\) times faster than the joint, its kinetic energy, written in joint velocity, carries the factor \(\GearReductionRatio^2\):

\[\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \RotorInertia \RotorAngularSpeed^2 = \frac{1}{2} \RotorInertia (\GearReductionRatio \JointVelocity)^2 = \frac{1}{2} (\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia) \JointVelocity^2\]

In precision robotic manipulators equipped with high-ratio strain-wave gearing7 (\(\GearReductionRatio = 50\text{--}100\)), reflected rotor inertia is magnified by a factor of \(2{,}500\) to \(10{,}000\). It can then rival or exceed link inertia, most often for an unloaded arm or a folded pose, although a rated payload held at full reach outweighs it. In the one-joint example the payload supplies most of the 1.65 kg·m² total, against 0.18 kg·m² from the rotor. Either way, the reflected term sits in every joint’s \(\EffectiveJointInertia\) regardless of payload, and on its own it makes seam torque spikes large. At a \(\DifferentiabilityClass^0\) boundary, an instantaneous velocity step implies an idealized Dirac acceleration impulse \(\UnitImpulseFunction(\TimeInstant)\), which no physical motor drive can supply. At a \(\DifferentiabilityClass^1\) boundary, an acceleration jump \(\FiniteChange\JointAcceleration\) demands a step change in electromagnetic torque, \(\FiniteChange\CommandedJointTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia \FiniteChange\JointAcceleration\). Such abrupt torque transients excite structural resonance in flexible transmissions, accelerate mechanical wear, and degrade control tracking. On the strain-wave joint priced in the notebook below, an unblended one-tick velocity step of 0.2 rad/s asks the reflected rotor inertia for a 36.7 N·m torque pulse, while a 50 ms \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) quintic bridge closing the same gap peaks at 1.1 N·m (figure 4). Commercial industrial arms enforce this exact discipline at their low-level control interfaces. For example, the Franka Research 3 arm samples setpoints at 1 kHz, comparing each incoming command against the previous cycle. If inter-cycle setpoint differences exceed factory derivative thresholds, the drive immediately aborts motion with an unrecoverable discontinuity fault (such as joint_motion_generator_velocity_discontinuity) (Franka Robotics GmbH 2026, 2023). An unblended seam can therefore abort nominal tracking before repeated transitions accumulate mechanical fatigue. Physical stopping still requires the drive’s qualified response delay and deceleration profile.

7 Strain-wave (harmonic) gearing: In the mechanism Musser (1959) patented, an elliptical wave generator flexes a thin toothed cup, the flexspline, so that it meshes with a rigid ring carrying two more teeth, and each turn of the generator advances the cup by only that difference. The result is a high reduction in one compact stage with almost no backlash. The flexspline is also the joint’s main compliance, so the same gearing that multiplies rotor inertia by \(\GearReductionRatio^2\) supplies the compliant coupling of the two-inertia model below.

Musser, C. Walton. 1959. Strain Wave Gearing. U.S. Patent 2,906,143, filed March 21, 1955, issued September 29, 1959.
Franka Robotics GmbH. 2026. Troubleshooting. Franka Control Interface documentation.

When the arm carries an open cup of liquid, steady velocity does not spill the contents, whereas rapid acceleration transitions and high jerk provoke sloshing and spillage (section 11.5.2 models this deceleration threshold directly). A trajectory interface that continuously matches position, velocity, and acceleration constitutes a \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) spline seam. Enforcing \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) continuity eliminates reference acceleration steps and prevents the associated torque jump \(\FiniteChange\CommandedJointTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia\FiniteChange\JointAcceleration\). Concurrently, bounding trajectory jerk (\(\JointJerk=\Glyph{\dot{a}}=\dddot{\Glyph{x}}\)) limits the rate at which joint acceleration changes. Neither mathematical property alone guarantees payload stability or mechanical longevity, however; an interpolating bridge that matches boundary conditions perfectly can still violate velocity, jerk, actuator torque, or obstacle clearance limits in its interior.

Mechanical transmissions govern how actuator torque couples into physical link motion. In a standard two-inertia drivetrain comprising load inertia \(\LoadInertia\), reflected rotor inertia \(\ReflectedRotorInertia=\GearReductionRatio^2\RotorInertia\), and torsional coupling stiffness \(\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness\), the antiresonance and resonance frequencies (Li et al. 2024) are:

Li, Peng, Zhenguo Nie, Zihao Li, and Xinjun Liu. 2024. “Optimal Design of the Modular Joint Drive Train for Enhancing Cobot Load Capacity and Dynamic Performance.” Chinese Journal of Mechanical Engineering 37 (1): 57. https://doi.org/10.1186/s10033-024-01041-5.

\[\DrivetrainAntiresonanceFrequency = \frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness}{\LoadInertia}}, \qquad \DrivetrainResonanceFrequency = \frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness\left(\frac{1}{\LoadInertia} + \frac{1}{\ReflectedRotorInertia}\right)}\]

In robot joints constructed with strain-wave gearing, large reflected inertia coupled with flexspline torsional compliance amplifies high-frequency setpoint harmonics. An admission checker must therefore verify prospective bridges against the joint’s identified, load-dependent vibrational modes, evaluating derivative extrema, peak torques, and drive current limits against empirical structural parameters. The two frequencies reach the checker through the bridge duration. A short bridge can excite these modes, but duration alone does not determine its spectral content or the joint’s response. Identified modal frequencies and damping therefore inform qualified bridge-duration and derivative limits alongside the torque-rate limit. Structural stiffness, resonance modes, and admissible jerk envelopes never transfer between robot platforms without direct system identification.

11.4.4 Quintic splines and C\(^2\) boundary matching

Closing the position, velocity, and acceleration gap between retiring chunk \(\Glyph{A}\) and incoming chunk \(\Glyph{B}\) over a finite blend duration \(\SeamBridgeDuration\) requires online \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) trajectory spline blending. The blend duration is a physical execution interval, distinct from the computational blend deadline \(\BlendDeadline\). Blending synthesizes a dedicated polynomial bridge \(\SeamBridgePosition(\TimeInstant)\), which must undergo the exact same kinodynamic feasibility checks as the primary chunks rather than acting as a cosmetic filter.8 A localized seam bridge must satisfy position, velocity, and acceleration constraints at both boundary states, which gives six independent boundary conditions. A cubic polynomial has only four degrees of freedom and cannot satisfy them. A fifth-degree quintic polynomial provides the six required coefficients. This localized endpoint-matching formulation differs from fitting a globally continuous cubic spline across an entire offline sequence of knots.

8 Minimum jerk: Flash and Hogan (1985) found that unconstrained human reaching follows the trajectory that minimizes integrated squared jerk, which for a rest-to-rest reach is a quintic polynomial in time. The jerk term of equation 20 inherits that smoothness but not a bound, so a minimum-jerk bridge can still exceed the drive’s limits, and its extrema are checked through equation 13.

Flash, Tamar, and Neville Hogan. 1985. “The Coordination of Arm Movements: An Experimentally Confirmed Mathematical Model.” The Journal of Neuroscience 5 (7): 1688–703. https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.05-07-01688.1985.

The six boundary conditions are \[\JointPosition(0) = \JointPosition_0, \quad \JointVelocity(0) = \JointVelocity_0, \quad \JointAcceleration(0) = \JointAcceleration_0\] \[\JointPosition(\SeamBridgeDuration) = \JointPosition_1, \quad \JointVelocity(\SeamBridgeDuration) = \JointVelocity_1, \quad \JointAcceleration(\SeamBridgeDuration) = \JointAcceleration_1\] where \((\JointPosition_0, \JointVelocity_0, \JointAcceleration_0)\) is the terminal physical state of chunk \(\Glyph{A}\) at the seam instant, and \((\JointPosition_1, \JointVelocity_1, \JointAcceleration_1)\) is the target state on incoming chunk \(\Glyph{B}\) at \(\TimeInstant = \SeamBridgeDuration\).
