The six conditions fix the six coefficients (\(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_0 \dots \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5\)) of the fifth-degree polynomial: \[\JointPosition(\TimeInstant) = \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_0 + \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_1 \TimeInstant + \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_2 \TimeInstant^2 + \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3 \TimeInstant^3 + \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4 \TimeInstant^4 + \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 \TimeInstant^5\]

Evaluating boundary conditions at \(\TimeInstant = 0\) directly yields the first three coefficients: \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_0 = \JointPosition_0\), \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_1 = \JointVelocity_0\), and \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_2 = \frac{1}{2}\JointAcceleration_0\). The remaining three coefficients \([\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3, \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4, \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5]^\top\) satisfy the \(3 \times 3\) linear system evaluated at duration \(\Time = \SeamBridgeDuration\):

\[\begin{bmatrix} \SeamBridgeDuration^3 & \SeamBridgeDuration^4 & \SeamBridgeDuration^5 \\ 3\SeamBridgeDuration^2 & 4\SeamBridgeDuration^3 & 5\SeamBridgeDuration^4 \\ 6\SeamBridgeDuration & 12\SeamBridgeDuration^2 & 20\SeamBridgeDuration^3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3 \\ \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4 \\ \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \JointPosition_1 - \left(\JointPosition_0 + \JointVelocity_0 \SeamBridgeDuration + \frac{1}{2}\JointAcceleration_0 \SeamBridgeDuration^2\right) \\ \JointVelocity_1 - \left(\JointVelocity_0 + \JointAcceleration_0 \SeamBridgeDuration\right) \\ \JointAcceleration_1 - \JointAcceleration_0 \end{bmatrix} \tag{12}\]

Analytically inverting equation 12 yields closed-form expressions for \([\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3, \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4, \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5]^\top\), given in Smooth Trajectories and Bumpless Transfer, that require deterministic arithmetic operations per joint. Differentiating \(\JointPosition(\TimeInstant)\) three times produces the continuous commanded jerk profile:

\[\JointJerk(\TimeInstant) = \dddot{\JointPosition}(\TimeInstant) = 6 \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3 + 24 \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4 \TimeInstant + 60 \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 \TimeInstant^2 \tag{13}\]

Because \(\JointJerk(\TimeInstant)\) in equation 13 is a quadratic function of time, its extrema over the interval \([0, \Time]\) occur exclusively at the domain boundaries (\(\TimeInstant = 0\) or \(\TimeInstant = \Time\)) or at the interior stationary point \(\TimeInstant^* = -\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4 / (5 \PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5)\). The interior point applies when \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 \ne 0\) and \(\TimeInstant^* \in (0, \Time)\). If \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 = 0\), jerk varies linearly or remains constant, and evaluating the boundaries alone suffices. The safety microcontroller can therefore compute exact peak jerk \(|\PeakBridgeJerk|\) analytically in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) operations without executing iterative numerical line searches. An identical principle governs lower derivatives: acceleration extrema occur at the roots of the quadratic jerk, and velocity extrema occur at the roots of cubic acceleration, both solvable in closed form.

Evaluating coupled joint torques across the bridge presents an entirely different challenge. Along a polynomial trajectory \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)\), the right-hand side of equation 8 is non-polynomial because the inertia matrix \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\), Coriolis matrix \(\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\), and gravity vector \(\GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) contain transcendental trigonometric functions of joint positions. Consequently, peak torque possesses no closed-form analytic solution. The safety monitor therefore evaluates torque at discrete sample waypoints and bounds inter-sample torque variation using the contraction principles established in section 11.4.2.

Bounding trajectory jerk directly protects actuator health, preserves motor inverter voltage headroom, and prevents premature transmission wear. When a trajectory generator outputs time-parameterized splines, it transforms kinematic waypoints into continuous reference functions whose derivatives map directly onto physical electrical and mechanical states. In permanent-magnet synchronous motors operated under field-oriented control, joint torque rate couples to jerk through the time derivative of the manipulator equation: \(\JointTorqueSlew(\TimeInstant) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \dddot{\GeneralizedCoordinates}(\TimeInstant) + \InertiaMatrixDerivative(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \GeneralizedAccelerations(\TimeInstant) + \dots\). For a single joint with constant effective inertia \(\EffectiveJointInertia = \LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\), neglecting changing gravity, friction, and external-load torques reduces the output torque rate to \(\CommandedJointTorqueSlew = \EffectiveJointInertia \JointJerk(\TimeInstant)\). Defining a joint-effective torque constant \(\JointTorqueConstant\) such that output torque satisfies \(\CommandedJointTorque = \JointTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\), commanding an output torque rate \(\CommandedJointTorqueSlew\) demands a proportional quadrature-axis stator current slew rate:

\[\frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} = \frac{\EffectiveJointInertia}{\JointTorqueConstant} \JointJerk(\TimeInstant)\]

Here, \(\JointTorqueConstant\) incorporates both the gear reduction ratio and transmission mechanical efficiency; directly substituting a raw motor-side torque constant without gear scaling would incorrectly pair motor torque with output-side inertia. Motor winding inductance \(\PhaseInductance\) and available DC bus voltage impose hard electrical limits on current slew rate. During positive motoring operations with resistive and back-electromotive force (back-EMF) drops, a simplified necessary condition for voltage headroom is:

\[\DcBusVoltage \ge \PhaseInductance \frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} + \PhaseResistance\, \TorqueProducingCurrent + \MotorBackEmfConstant \ElectricalAngularSpeed \tag{14}\]

where \(\PhaseResistance\) represents phase winding resistance, \(\MotorBackEmfConstant\) is the motor back-EMF constant, and \(\ElectricalAngularSpeed\) denotes motor electrical speed. In operational motor drives, space-vector pulse-width modulation limits the maximum usable quadrature-axis voltage to a fraction of the total DC bus voltage. A complete electrical feasibility check must evaluate available voltage against the fully coupled, signed motor equations, including regenerative braking and inductive flyback, rather than treat a scalar approximation as sufficient qualification.

↩︎ Phase inductance, back-EMF, and the current-slew limits they impose are established in Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping.

An acceleration discontinuity in a motion plan demands an instantaneous step in electromagnetic torque and stator current. Because finite bus voltage and nonzero winding inductance prevent physical inverters from supplying infinite current slew rates (\(d\TorqueProducingCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)), the physical actuator lags the commanded reference. The resulting tracking error, inverter saturation, and thermal protection response depend on drive gains and mechanical compliance. An unbounded mathematical jerk demand does not instantly cause an overcurrent shutdown, but it induces severe tracking lag, mechanical vibration, and drive stress. A valid bridge must satisfy both continuous mathematical derivative bounds and the physical bandwidth limits of the actuator drive.

Satisfying the six boundary conditions guarantees acceleration continuity at chunk interfaces, but it does not guarantee that interior jerk, torque, or current slew rates remain within actuator ratings. The admission filter evaluates closed-form derivative extrema from equation 13 against the electrical constraints of equation 14, checks sampled multi-body torques against inter-sample contraction bounds, and rejects any bridge violating physical thresholds. Evaluating a fixed-degree polynomial requires bounded arithmetic operations per joint. Spline coefficient synthesis and trajectory extrema verification take place prior to trajectory dispatch; the qualified worst-case execution bound, accounting for memory access latency and register operations, must remain strictly within the configured admission deadline.

While a local quintic bridge resolves seam continuity through explicit boundary matching, global trajectory representations provide alternative verification properties. Uniform B-splines9 parameterize joint trajectories as \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant) = \sum_{i=0}^\SplineFinalControlIndex \SplineControlPoint_i {\BsplineBasisFunction}_{i,\SplineDegree}(\TimeInstant)\), where \(\SplineControlPoint_i\) represent geometric control points, \({\BsplineBasisFunction}_{i,\SplineDegree}\) are polynomial basis functions of degree \(\SplineDegree\), and \(\SplineKnotTime_i\) denote knot timestamps. Using simple knot sequences, uniform B-splines ensure \(\DifferentiabilityClass^{\SplineDegree-1}\) continuity by construction. Furthermore, the convex hull property ensures that the continuous trajectory lies entirely within the convex hull formed by its control points, enabling spatial collision checkers to prune entire collision volumes without dense sampling. Differentiating a B-spline yields a derivative spline whose control points are \(\DerivativeSplineControlPoint_i = \frac{\SplineDegree}{\SplineKnotTime_{i+\SplineDegree+1}-\SplineKnotTime_{i+1}}(\SplineControlPoint_{i+1} - \SplineControlPoint_i)\), allowing difference operations on control points to establish conservative upper bounds on velocity, acceleration, and jerk. These geometric bounds accelerate screening, though they do not replace full dynamic inverse dynamics or swept-volume collision checks. In streaming replanning architectures, the trajectory representation must also support rapid coefficient joining within the real-time admission deadline; table 1 separates the continuity supplied by each representation from the dynamic checks still required before execution.

9 Spline (a draftsman’s bending strip): The word named the thin flexible strip that shipwrights and draftsmen bent through fixed points, held in place by lead weights, to draw a fair curve. Schoenberg (1946) borrowed it for piecewise polynomials, and Boor (1978) made B-splines practical to compute with a stable recursion. The convex-hull property that construction supplies is what lets a checker bound a whole segment’s derivatives from a few control-point differences.

Schoenberg, Isaac J. 1946. “Contributions to the Problem of Approximation of Equidistant Data by Analytic Functions. Part A. On the Problem of Smoothing or Graduation. A First Class of Analytic Approximation Formulae.” Quarterly of Applied Mathematics 4 (1): 45–99. https://doi.org/10.1090/qam/15914.
Boor, Carl de. 1978. A Practical Guide to Splines. Vol. 27. Applied Mathematical Sciences. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4612-6333-3.

Napkin Math 11.2: The seam spike
Problem: At a replanning seam the incoming chunk’s reference runs 0.2 rad/s faster than the active one. Should the joint close that gap in one servo tick or over a \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) bridge, and what does each ask of the drive?

Variables:

  • Joint: a CSG-25-50 strain-wave gear driven by a TQ-RoboDrive ILM 70x18 motor, a Panda-class stand-in because Franka does not publish the Panda’s joint internals (datasheet parts); reflected rotor inertia \(\EffectiveJointInertia = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia =\) 0.18 kg·m² (derived; link and payload inertia excluded, so here \(\EffectiveJointInertia\) is the reflected inertia \(\ReflectedRotorInertia\) of Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching alone)
  • Ratings 51 N·m rated and 127 N·m repeated peak (datasheet); the arm’s torque-rate limit 1,000 N·m/s (datasheet)
  • Velocity gap \(\FiniteChange\JointVelocity =\) 0.2 rad/s (illustrative) and servo tick \(\SamplingPeriod =\) 1 ms (chosen)
  • Bridge duration \(\SeamBridgeDuration =\) 50 ms (illustrative), zero acceleration at both ends, ending on chunk B’s reference, a position residual \(\FiniteChange\JointVelocity\,\SeamBridgeDuration/2 =\) 0.005 rad (chosen)

Math:

  1. One tick (equation 11): \(\EffectiveJointInertia\,\FiniteChange\JointVelocity/\SamplingPeriod =\) 36.7 N·m, below the rating. Reaching it within the tick needs at least 36.7 N·m per 1 ms, or 36,700 N·m/s, 36.7× the torque-rate limit.
  2. Quintic bridge (equation 12): Because a symmetric velocity transition from \(\JointVelocity_0\) to \(\JointVelocity_0 + \FiniteChange\JointVelocity\) averages \(\JointVelocity_0 + \frac{1}{2}\FiniteChange\JointVelocity\) across the bridge, the joint advances by \(\FiniteChange\JointVelocity\,\SeamBridgeDuration/2\) beyond chunk A’s constant-velocity extrapolation; with that position residual and zero boundary accelerations, the system gives \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_5 = 0\), \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_4=-\FiniteChange\JointVelocity/(2\SeamBridgeDuration^3)=-800\ \text{rad/s}^4\), and \(\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3=\FiniteChange\JointVelocity/\SeamBridgeDuration^2=80\ \text{rad/s}^3\).
  3. Bridge peaks: Acceleration peaks at mid-bridge at \(1.5\,\FiniteChange\JointVelocity/\SeamBridgeDuration =\) 6 rad/s², a torque of 1.1 N·m. Jerk (equation 13) peaks at both ends at \(6\PolynomialTrajectoryCoefficient_3 =\) 480 rad/s³, a torque rate \(\EffectiveJointInertia\,\JointJerk\) of 88.1 N·m/s, 8.8 percent of the limit.
  4. Half the duration: Peak torque scales as \(1/\SeamBridgeDuration\) and jerk as \(1/\SeamBridgeDuration^2\), so a bridge half as long needs 2.2 N·m, 1,920 rad/s³, and 352.3 N·m/s, still inside the limit.

Result: The one-tick step passes a torque check and asks for 36.7× the torque-rate limit; the bridge passes both with a 33.3× smaller torque peak.

Systems insight: Evaluating torque magnitude alone is an inadequate feasibility screen at a trajectory seam. Actuator drives fail on the rate of change of torque, which grows quadratically (\(1/\SeamBridgeDuration^2\)) as blending duration compresses. When late trajectory admission forces the system to compress bridge duration \(\SeamBridgeDuration\), required jerk and torque slew rates increase fourfold for every twofold reduction in duration. An admission monitor must explicitly re-evaluate the compressed bridge rather than inheriting validity from the uncompressed nominal profile. Furthermore, link inertia, payload mass, gravity, and joint friction compound this inertial baseline, requiring independent peak torque and thermal budget verification. The torque verdict of step 1 depends on that exclusion. With the payload inertia of equation 7 included, 1.65 kg·m² in place of 0.18 kg·m², the one-tick request grows by the same ratio and exceeds even the 127 N·m repeated peak, so the one-tick step fails the torque check as well.

High-frequency chatter in learned policy outputs represents an identical physical hazard under a different guise. Stochastic sampling policies, such as diffusion policies or action-chunking transformers, often generate consecutive trajectory chunks where individual waypoints satisfy static position and velocity limits, yet commanded accelerations reverse sign erratically between steps. Each acceleration reversal demands an immediate torque reversal from the actuator, triggering rapid current transitions and severe \(\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \PhaseResistance\) resistive heating in the motor windings. In physical AI systems, engineers must treat this high-frequency variance as a direct cause of thermal rise and transmission fatigue rather than cosmetic jitter. A trajectory candidate that excites structural resonances or exceeds drive thermal dissipation limits is infeasible, regardless of how closely its geometric path resembles demonstration data.

11.4.5 Time parameterization under torque and jerk bounds

A collision-free curve through configuration space says where the arm goes. It says nothing about how fast the arm may travel along that curve without saturating a joint’s torque limit on the way up or demanding an impossible torque step when it switches to braking. Geometric search and neural proposal generators determine only the spatial path of motion: a curve \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\PathCoordinate)\) parameterized by scalar path coordinate \(\PathCoordinate \in [0, 1]\). The timing law \(\PathCoordinate(\TimeInstant)\) governs the physical motion, determining every joint velocity, acceleration, and torque required along the trajectory. Establishing a valid timing profile \(\PathCoordinate(\TimeInstant)\) subject to physical actuator limits is the problem of time parameterization. Applying the chain rule to the joint coordinates yields:

\[\GeneralizedVelocities = \JointPathTangent(\PathCoordinate)\,\PathVelocity, \qquad \GeneralizedAccelerations = \JointPathTangent(\PathCoordinate)\,\PathAcceleration + \JointPathCurvature(\PathCoordinate)\,\PathVelocity^2\]

where primes denote differentiation with respect to path coordinate \(\PathCoordinate\) (\(\DerivativeOperator\GeneralizedCoordinates/d\PathCoordinate\) and \(\DerivativeOperator^2\GeneralizedCoordinates/d\PathCoordinate^2\)). Substituting these path derivatives into the unconstrained manipulator equation from equation 8 expresses joint torque as a function of three path-dependent terms:

\[\JointTorque = \PathAccelerationTorqueCoefficient(\PathCoordinate)\,\PathAcceleration + \PathVelocitySquaredTorqueCoefficient(\PathCoordinate)\,\PathVelocity^2 + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\PathCoordinate) \tag{15}\]

with vector coefficients \(\PathAccelerationTorqueCoefficient(\PathCoordinate) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\JointPathTangent(\PathCoordinate)\), \(\PathVelocitySquaredTorqueCoefficient(\PathCoordinate) = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\JointPathCurvature(\PathCoordinate) + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates,\JointPathTangent)\,\JointPathTangent(\PathCoordinate)\), and \(\GravitationalTorqueVector(\PathCoordinate) = \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\). Each actuator torque bound \(|\CommandedJointTorque_i| \le {\JointTorqueLimit}_i\) translates into an admissible interval of path acceleration, \(\PathAccelerationLowerBound(\PathCoordinate,\PathVelocity) \le \PathAcceleration \le \PathAccelerationUpperBound(\PathCoordinate,\PathVelocity)\), at every coordinate of the phase plane \((\PathCoordinate, \PathVelocity)\). Actuator velocity and acceleration limits introduce equivalent bounding intervals. As path velocity \(\PathVelocity\) increases, the centrifugal and Coriolis term \(\PathVelocitySquaredTorqueCoefficient(\PathCoordinate)\PathVelocity^2\) grows quadratically until the admissible acceleration interval closes entirely (\(\PathAccelerationLowerBound = \PathAccelerationUpperBound\)). The locus of phase-plane points where this interval collapses defines the maximum velocity curve, representing an upper speed envelope that no feasible parameterization can exceed.

Because total motion duration is \(\Time = \int_0^1 \frac{1}{\PathVelocity}\,d\PathCoordinate\), the minimum-time profile maintains the highest permissible path velocity \(\PathVelocity\) at every point along the path. Under torque constraints alone, the time-optimal solution takes a bang-bang form (Bobrow et al. 1985). The system accelerates along the upper bound \(\PathAccelerationUpperBound\) until its phase-plane trajectory encounters a braking curve integrated backward from the goal at lower bound \(\PathAccelerationLowerBound\). It then decelerates along \(\PathAccelerationLowerBound\) to reach the target at rest. Switch points mark the phase-plane coordinates where the trajectory transitions between maximum acceleration and maximum braking. The TOPP-RA algorithm (Pham and Pham 2018) solves this phase-plane parameterization efficiently by transforming it into a convex optimization problem. By substituting squared path velocity \(\SquaredPathVelocity = \PathVelocity^2\) and path acceleration \(\PathAccelerationInput = \PathAcceleration\), the torque constraints in equation 15 and the differential step equation \(\SquaredPathVelocity_{i+1} = \SquaredPathVelocity_i + 2\,\FiniteChange \PathCoordinate\,\PathAccelerationInput_i\) become linear across a grid of \(\PathGridPointCount\) path waypoints. (This linear step relation follows from \(d\SquaredPathVelocity/d\PathCoordinate = 2\PathVelocity\,(\DerivativeOperator\PathVelocity/d\PathCoordinate) = 2\PathAcceleration\), matching the kinematic equation \(\Glyph{v}^2 = 2\Glyph{a} \Glyph{d}\)). A backward integration pass computes the controllable interval of \(\SquaredPathVelocity\) at each grid point from which the terminal state remains reachable, solving a single linear program per point. A subsequent forward pass selects the largest admissible acceleration \(\PathAccelerationInput\) at each step. The complete pass runs in \(\mathcal{O}(\PathGridPointCount)\) time, and an empty feasible interval at any point provides constructive proof that no parameterization satisfies the bounds on that grid. Kinematic variants that constrain only joint velocity and acceleration compute this timing structure with lower overhead (Kunz and Stilman 2012). The Kunz and Stilman algorithm serves as the default time-parameterization method in MoveIt 2, which can apply a downstream jerk-limiting filter (MoveIt 2 Documentation 2026).

Kunz, Tobias, and Mike Stilman. 2012. “Time-Optimal Trajectory Generation for Path Following with Bounded Acceleration and Velocity.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS) VIII. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2012.VIII.027.
MoveIt 2 Documentation. 2026. Time Parameterization. MoveIt 2 tutorials, PickNik Robotics.

Napkin Math 11.3: Time-optimal swing of one joint
Problem: The shoulder lifts the rated payload at full planar reach through 0.5 rad, from rest to rest. How fast can it swing, and what does the fastest timing ask of the drive?

Variables:

  • Swing \(\FiniteChange \JointPosition =\) 0.5 rad (illustrative), with the joint angle itself as the path parameter (\(\PathCoordinate = \JointPosition\), measured from the start)
  • Inertia \(\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 1.65 kg·m² and payload gravity torque \(\PayloadGravityTorque =\) 20.6 N·m, held at its horizontal value (derived, payload only)
  • Joint limits: torque \(\JointTorqueLimit =\) 87 N·m, acceleration 7.5 rad/s², velocity 2.175 rad/s, torque rate \(\TorqueRateLimit =\) 1000 N·m/s (datasheet (Franka Robotics GmbH 2023))

Math:

  1. Binding acceleration: The torque limit allows \((\JointTorqueLimit-\PayloadGravityTorque)/\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 40.2 rad/s² upward and \((\JointTorqueLimit+\PayloadGravityTorque)/\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 65.1 rad/s² braking, an asymmetry that gravity creates. The published 7.5 rad/s² is tighter than both, so it binds in both directions.
  2. Time-optimal swing: The accelerating curve \(\PathVelocity^2 = 2\ProfileAccelerationBound \PathCoordinate\) meets the braking curve at mid-swing, where speed peaks at \(\sqrt{\ProfileAccelerationBound\,\FiniteChange \JointPosition} =\) 1.94 rad/s, below the velocity limit. The swing takes \(2\sqrt{\FiniteChange \JointPosition/\ProfileAccelerationBound} =\) 516.4 ms.
  3. Switch-point step: Acceleration reverses from \(+\ProfileAccelerationBound\) to \(-\ProfileAccelerationBound\), a torque step \(2\EffectiveJointInertia{}\ProfileAccelerationBound =\) 24.8 N·m, which needs at least 24.8 ms at the torque-rate limit. That limit bounds jerk at \(\TorqueRateLimit/\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 604.8 rad/s³.
  4. Jerk-limited swing: A rest-to-rest double-S profile with no cruise covers \(\FiniteChange \JointPosition = \ProfileAccelerationBound\,\SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime\,(\SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime + \ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit)\) in \(\Time = 2(\SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime + \ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit)\). Because the accelerating half of the swing consists of a constant-acceleration hold framed by two jerk ramps of duration \(\ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit\) (one ramping acceleration up to \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) and one ramping it back to zero at mid-swing), \(\SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime = \Time/2 - \ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit\) is half the swing less one jerk ramp (the constant-acceleration hold plus one ramp), giving the trapezoidal acceleration pulse an area equal to the peak speed \(\ProfileAccelerationBound \SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime\). With \(\ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit =\) 12.4 ms, the swing needs \(\SCurveAccelerationPhaseTime =\) 252.1 ms and takes 528.9 ms.

Systems insight: Enforcing the torque-rate limit incurs an execution penalty of 12.6 ms, a modest 2.4 percent increase in motion duration, while eliminating the instantaneous torque step entirely. For the payload alone, torque was not the binding constraint; the published kinematic limits governed the trajectory, though identified link inertia would narrow this torque headroom. A valid time parameterization must incorporate every constraint capable of binding in practice. The theoretical time-optimal profile without a jerk bound is an unexecutable mathematical abstraction that physical drives reject.

Franka Robotics GmbH. 2023. Robot and Interface Specifications. Franka Control Interface documentation.

In the phase plane the two profiles differ only near their corners (figure 5). The torque limits alone would permit steeper accelerating and braking parabolas, but the published 7.5 rad/s² acceleration limit contracts both arcs, and the bang-bang path rides them to its 1.94 rad/s switch point, well below the velocity limit. The jerk-limited path rounds the start, the apex, and the finish just inside those arcs. That rounding is where its extra 2.4 percent of duration goes, and it is what removes the 24.8 N·m torque step at the switch point.

The time-optimal bang-bang solution produces precisely the derivative discontinuities that safe seam execution forbids. Every transition between maximum acceleration and maximum braking represents a step change in acceleration, demanding an instantaneous step in electromagnetic torque that actuator drives cannot deliver within finite voltage and current slew limits. Incorporating an explicit jerk bound transforms the problem into a third-order trajectory formulation. As the shoulder swing notebook shows, the time cost of bounding jerk is small next to the torque step it removes. Modern motion pipelines therefore parameterize paths under coupled torque and velocity limits and enforce explicit jerk bounds downstream, or constrain jerk directly during parameterization.

An identical jerk-limited profile, synthesized from the robot’s verified state, resolves both seam blending and safe stopping. An online trajectory generator receives the instantaneous position, velocity, and acceleration of each joint together with a target boundary state, returning a time-optimal profile that satisfies per-axis velocity, acceleration, and jerk limits (Kröger and Wahl 2010). For example, the Ruckig algorithm (Berscheid and Kröger 2021) evaluates arbitrary initial and target states across seven distinct constant-jerk phases, calculating profile durations analytically for most trajectory classes and using a bounded polynomial root solver for asymmetric edge cases. In published benchmarks, Ruckig achieved an average execution latency of 19.8 μs and a worst-case latency of 123 μs for 7 axes on a desktop processor, making it fast enough to re-solve within a 1 kHz control cycle. This formulation differs structurally from the fixed-duration quintic bridge in section 11.4.4. The quintic approach fixes blending duration \(\SeamBridgeDuration\) upfront and risks exceeding joint limits during aggressive maneuvers, so downstream extrema checks must reject invalid solutions. An online trajectory generator instead solves for minimum duration as an optimization variable, which guarantees that kinematic derivative bounds hold by construction. The S-curve stopping trajectory examined in section 11.5.2 represents this exact profile structure evaluated toward a zero-velocity terminal state.

Kinematic feasibility by construction, however, does not grant permission to move. An online generator guarantees only the mathematical bounds explicitly configured in its parameters. Single-axis velocity, acceleration, and jerk bounds cannot account for multi-joint dynamic coupling in equation 8, spatial swept-volume collisions, or stale perception data. Furthermore, configuration errors or numerical solver faults can directly compromise the guarantees the generator claims to provide. Real-time benchmarks reported on high-performance desktop CPUs also do not represent execution latency on a constrained safety MCU. Online generators produce higher-quality proposals that satisfy kinematic constraints by design, but the deterministic safety controller must independently validate dynamic feasibility, collision margins, and temporal deadlines before granting execution permission. Even a candidate trajectory that passes every internal dynamic and seam continuity check remains unsafe without an admitted contingency. The system must therefore continuously maintain a certified stopping suffix to handle replacement chunks that fail to arrive before the admission deadline.

Checkpoint 11.2: Followable motion and the seam

A collision-free candidate becomes followable only after its joint rates, its seams, and its timing fit the body’s limits.

Mapping and sampling

Pricing the seam

11.5 Behavior at the Seam

When the replacement does not arrive, because inference stalls on scheduling interference, a bus fault, or an unhandled exception, the arm reaches the end of its authority with no successor in hand. The runtime must then bring it to rest without violating the limits derived above or exhausting its stopping clearance. Three things decide the outcome: which deadline has passed, which stop the MCU runs, and how much clearance that stop consumes.

11.5.1 Replacement deadlines and commitment cutoffs

A single timeout cannot protect both a fast control loop against a hung proposer and a moving arm against overrunning its obstacle clearance. A lease short enough to catch a dropped packet fires long before the geometric point of no return. A cutoff placed at the point of no return leaves a stalled policy coasting blind in the meantime. The two tiers named with the trajectory record in section 11.2, the periodic chunk lease and the commitment cutoff, therefore answer different questions: whether a replacement has renewed tracking authority recently enough, and whether the reserved stop can still begin in time. The planner also uses a soft blend deadline (\(\BlendDeadline\)), a scheduling target for a smooth replacement. Missing that target need not end tracking if the current lease and evidence remain valid and a checked replacement can still arrive before the admission cutoff that precedes physical commitment (\(\CommitmentInstant\)). The earlier admission cutoff leaves the response interval needed for the matched stopping suffix to begin at commitment, before its reserved clearance is spent.

The two deadlines call for two different stops:

  • Matched stop suffix (planned termination at \(\TimeInstant = \CommitmentInstant\)): A pre-computed, jerk-limited \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) slowdown along the admitted path, anchored specifically at the nominal state expected at \(\CommitmentInstant\), checked at admission to satisfy \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). If no successor is admitted before the earlier admission cutoff, the controller dispatches the stop early enough that the body smoothly transfers to this pre-latched suffix at commitment, decelerating to rest without requiring runtime replanning.
  • State-matched fallback (reactive termination at \(\TimeInstant < \CommitmentInstant\)): If the high-rate chunk lease lapses, the parent intent expires, or a runtime safety monitor detects an unmodeled obstacle or torque fault before reaching \(\CommitmentInstant\), the robot cannot use the matched stop suffix (which is anchored at the future state \(\CommitmentInstant\)). Instead, the MCU selects or constructs a validated state-matched fallback from current state estimates \((\JointPosition, \JointVelocity, \JointAcceleration)\) within reserved clearance. Encoder-derived velocity and acceleration have their own error bounds; bounded polynomial evaluation does not eliminate sensing, dispatch, or actuator response delay.

Both are the stopping suffix that every admitted trajectory carries (definition 11.1), differing only in the entry state from which they start. For the running mug reach, a stalled proposer expires the shared lease 60 ms after the last admission. The MCU then requests its state-matched fallback; physical onset follows the separately budgeted response delay. All timeout checks use the MCU monotonic clock. The longer-lease comparison isolates the geometric limit: even with tracking evidence continually renewed, the controller must dispatch the matched stop early enough to begin at commitment. A host progress report changes neither cutoff. Both stops also inherit the seam’s continuity requirement, and that requirement fixes their shape and therefore their length.

11.5.2 The C\(^2\) stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking

A moving robot carries continuous momentum. If an emergency brake uses an idealized constant-deceleration profile (\(\StopAxisAcceleration(\TimeInstant) = -\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\)), the commanded acceleration steps discontinuously from zero to \(-\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) at \(\CommitmentInstant\), and steps discontinuously back to zero when velocity reaches zero. This instantaneous switch demands an infinite jerk impulse (\(\dddot{\StopAxisPosition} = \infty\)) and an instantaneous step jump in actuator braking torque (\(\FiniteChange\CommandedJointTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\)). A real drive can only approximate that step. Whether the response loses traction, disturbs the payload, or exceeds structural limits depends on the load and controller, so the worked design chooses an explicitly smooth profile.

Two bounds describe what the load and the drive tolerate during that stop: a peak deceleration \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) and a peak jerk \(\JerkLimit\), the rate at which deceleration may change. A \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stop keeps position, velocity, and acceleration continuous at both ends, so it must ramp its deceleration up from zero and back down to zero, and the jerk bound limits how quickly it can do either. Within those bounds, the shape of the profile decides how far the machine travels. Consider a cruise entry with position \(\StopAxisPosition(\CommitmentInstant) = \CommitmentPathPosition\), cruise speed \(\CruiseSpeed > 0\), and zero entry acceleration. One smooth candidate is the lowest-order polynomial that matches position, velocity, and acceleration at both ends, written in the normalized time \(\StopProgress = (\TimeInstant - \CommitmentInstant) / \StoppingDuration \in [0, 1]\):

\[\StopAxisPosition(\StopProgress) = \CommitmentPathPosition + \CruiseSpeed \StoppingDuration \left(\StopProgress - \StopProgress^3 + \frac{1}{2} \StopProgress^4\right), \qquad \StopAxisAcceleration(\StopProgress) = -\frac{6 \CruiseSpeed}{\StoppingDuration} \StopProgress(1 - \StopProgress), \qquad \dddot{\StopAxisPosition}(\StopProgress) = -\frac{6 \CruiseSpeed}{\StoppingDuration^2} (1 - 2\StopProgress) \tag{16}\]

Its deceleration is an inverted parabola that peaks at the midpoint, \(\PeakStopDeceleration = 1.5\,\CruiseSpeed/\StoppingDuration\), and its jerk is largest at the two ends, \(6\CruiseSpeed/\StoppingDuration^2\). Holding the peak at \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) gives \(\StoppingDuration = 1.5\,\CruiseSpeed/\ProfileAccelerationBound\) and a distance of \(\frac{1}{2}\CruiseSpeed \StoppingDuration = 0.75\,\CruiseSpeed^2/\ProfileAccelerationBound\), which is \(1.5\) times the constant-deceleration distance \(\CruiseSpeed^2/2\ProfileAccelerationBound\). That factor is a property of this polynomial, not of smooth stopping. The polynomial touches its peak deceleration only at the midpoint and spends half the stop below three quarters of it, so it gives away distance that its own bounds would allow it to use.

The profile that uses those bounds fully is the jerk-limited S-curve (trapezoidal acceleration, the “double S” velocity profile) (Biagiotti and Melchiorri 2008). It ramps deceleration from zero to \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) at the jerk limit, holds \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\), and ramps back to zero at the jerk limit just as velocity reaches zero. Each ramp lasts \(\ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit\). The velocity profile is point-symmetric about its midpoint, so the distance is half the entry speed times the duration:

\[\StoppingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) = \begin{cases} \dfrac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2\ProfileAccelerationBound} + \dfrac{\SpeedAtDetection\,\ProfileAccelerationBound}{2\JerkLimit}, & \SpeedAtDetection \ge \ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit \\[1.2ex] \SpeedAtDetection\sqrt{\SpeedAtDetection/\JerkLimit}, & \SpeedAtDetection < \ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit \end{cases} \qquad \StoppingDuration(\SpeedAtDetection) = \begin{cases} \dfrac{\SpeedAtDetection}{\ProfileAccelerationBound} + \dfrac{\ProfileAccelerationBound}{\JerkLimit}, & \SpeedAtDetection \ge \ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit \\[1.2ex] 2\sqrt{\SpeedAtDetection/\JerkLimit}, & \SpeedAtDetection < \ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit \end{cases} \tag{17}\]

Above the threshold speed \(\ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit\), the stop costs constant-deceleration braking at \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) plus cruise over half of one jerk ramp, \(\ProfileAccelerationBound/2\JerkLimit\), so a finite jerk limit acts like extra reaction time. Below the threshold the stop is too short to reach \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\), and its deceleration peaks at \(\sqrt{\SpeedAtDetection\,\JerkLimit}\). Among stops that respect both bounds and never reverse, this profile reaches rest in the least time and the least distance. The shape of the stop, not physics, sets the distance beyond \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/2\ProfileAccelerationBound\), and for given deceleration and jerk bounds the S-curve spends the least. A slower profile remains admissible, but it must be charged at its own distance.

The jerk bound belongs to the load. The loaded tote rack and the mug tolerate a deceleration only if it is reached gradually, and the registry does not yet hold a measured jerk tolerance for either. The worked design therefore sets each body’s \(\JerkLimit\) to the peak jerk of equation 16 at that body’s peak deceleration and top speed, a jerk the polynomial already asked the load to accept. A loaded stopping trial that measures the tolerable jerk would replace this stipulation, and equation 17 would then give the distance directly.

The stop is also a slowdown along the admitted path, not a new path. The planner fixes the geometry \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\PathCoordinate)\) it checked for clearance, and the S-curve governs only the speed \(\PathVelocity(\TimeInstant)\) along that geometry. The stop therefore sweeps no volume outside the corridor already checked, and the one-dimensional comparison \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) applies along the path. A stop computed separately for each joint would leave that path and would need its own swept-volume check. A nonzero measured entry acceleration requires a profile matched to the actual \((\JointPosition,\JointVelocity,\JointAcceleration)\) at entry with zero terminal velocity and acceleration; the permission path checks its interior speed, acceleration, jerk, torque, and clearance.

In the alternate longer-lease arm case, the blend deadline is 240 ms, matched commitment 300 ms, nominal setpoint end 400 ms, and stop end 575 ms, so the stop lasts 275 ms. At the arm’s 1 m/s TCP speed limit, the S-curve suffix with a 5 m/s² deceleration bound and a 66.7 m/s³ jerk bound travels 137.5 mm, compared with 150 mm for the polynomial of equation 16 with the same peaks. Because the blend deadline is the preferred admission instant on this schedule, the last scheduled validation and clearance evidence finish at 240 ms. The 60 mm of cruise between 240 ms and physical brake onset at 300 ms must therefore be charged against that clearance alongside the stop. Together they consume 197.5 mm in all, leaving 102.5 mm of the 300 mm obstacle clearance before sensing and model error are deducted. Because 300 ms is physical suffix onset, the MCU must close replacement admission and pretrigger any communication or actuator delay \(\UnreservedStopDelay\) by that instant minus \(\UnreservedStopDelay\). Otherwise, the arm travels an extra \(\CruiseSpeed\UnreservedStopDelay\), shifts the stop end later, and shrinks the 102.5 mm margin. Nominal setpoints end at 400 ms while MCU STOP continues to 575 ms, assuming fresh target evidence keeps TRACK valid until stop dispatch (section 11.3).

On the running machine, as the alternate schedule’s introduction noted, a stall never reaches commitment because the chunk lease lapses first, so its stop is the state-matched fallback. From zero-acceleration cruise the S-curve matches the arm at whatever instant it starts, and it slows the tool along the path already checked, so the state-matched fallback travels the same distance as the matched stop suffix. On the arm, as on the base, the chunk lease is a term of the stopping budget, the rule established in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget; the arm’s own speed, stop, and clearance set what that term costs.

Napkin Math 11.4: The stall runway
Problem: The proposer stalls while the arm reaches at its TCP limit, and no replacement is admitted. How much of the obstacle clearance does the arm use between its last admission and rest, and how much remains?

Variables:

  • Chunk lease \(\ChunkLease =\) 60 ms (chosen); permission tick 1 ms (derived) and bus cycle 1 ms (illustrative); onset 20 ms (illustrative, the base’s registered brake onset charged conservatively to the arm)
  • Speed \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1 m/s (chosen, TCP limit)
  • Deceleration bound \(\ProfileAccelerationBound =\) 5 m/s² (illustrative) and jerk bound \(\JerkLimit =\) 66.7 m/s³ (derived, the peak jerk of equation 16 at the same peak)
  • Clearance \(\ClearDistance =\) 300 mm (illustrative)

Math:

  1. Response path: 60 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 20 ms \(=\) 82 ms from last admission to stop onset, which is 82 mm at \(\SpeedAtDetection\).
  2. Branch test (equation 17): \(\ProfileAccelerationBound^2/\JerkLimit =\) 0.375 m/s, below \(\SpeedAtDetection\), so the stop reaches \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\).
  3. Stop: \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/2\ProfileAccelerationBound + \SpeedAtDetection\,\ProfileAccelerationBound/2\JerkLimit =\) 100 mm \(+\) 37.5 mm \(=\) 137.5 mm, in \(\SpeedAtDetection/\ProfileAccelerationBound + \ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit =\) 200 ms \(+\) 75 ms \(=\) 275 ms.
  4. Clearance: 82 mm \(+\) 137.5 mm \(=\) 219.5 mm of 300 mm.

Result: The stall leaves 80.5 mm of geometric margin, before sensing and model reserves.

Systems insight: The travel before the stop begins is 59.6 percent of the stop itself, and at this speed every millisecond of lease costs a millimeter. The remaining margin equals only 80.5 ms of further lease, so the lease length is a term of \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) exactly as the deceleration bound is.

While hardware qualification must establish the arm’s actual response in place of the charged onset allowance, the two schedules leave different geometric margins because they date their clearance evidence differently (figure 2). The running lease charges travel from the last admission (82 mm) plus the 137.5 mm stop, which keeps 80.5 mm. The alternate schedule charges only the cruise from its blend deadline, when clearance was last checked, to commitment (60 mm) plus the same stop, which keeps 102.5 mm. The alternate schedule’s 278 ms admission cutoff is therefore an offset on its own time axis rather than a lease length that the running clearance could support. Any lease, whatever its length, must fit its own evidence age, response travel, stop, and uncertainty reserve, just as the running lease does in the notebook above.

Although the free-space deceleration bound suits the empty-gripper reach toward the mug, it is too aggressive once the arm carries an open mug of liquid away from the conveyor, because container acceleration tilts the free surface and excites sloshing (Mayer and Krechetnikov 2012). Under a steady deceleration \(\SteadyDeceleration\), the free surface settles perpendicular to the effective gravity, tilting by \(\tan\FreeSurfaceTiltAngle = \SteadyDeceleration/\Gravity\) and raising the liquid at the wall by \(\ContainerInnerRadius\,\SteadyDeceleration/\Gravity\) for inner radius \(\ContainerInnerRadius\). In an illustrative mug of 40 mm inner radius filled to 10 mm below the rim, the free-space stop’s 5 m/s² deceleration raises the liquid 20.4 mm at the wall, well past the rim. Keeping even the steady tilt inside the freeboard \(\FreeboardHeight\) (a necessary condition, since dynamic sloshing overshoots the steady tilt) limits deceleration to \(\Gravity\,\FreeboardHeight/\ContainerInnerRadius\), or 2.45 m/s². At that gentler bound, with the jerk the polynomial reaches at the same peak, the stop from the 1 m/s TCP limit lengthens to 280.4 mm and, together with the running lease path, demands 362.4 mm of the 300 mm clearance. The loaded carry must therefore run slower than the empty reach. Before sensing and model reserves are deducted, the highest speed whose lease path and stop fit that clearance is 0.89 m/s. The trajectory record’s assumption sidecar must declare which payload regime and measured slosh limit (or lid) the record assumes.

Mayer, Hans C., and Rouslan Krechetnikov. 2012. “Walking with Coffee: Why Does It Spill?” Physical Review E 85 (4): 046117. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevE.85.046117.

At the TCP limit, the open mug’s gentler stop overruns the clearance the empty reach fits.

The commitment deadline forbids waiting. In the alternate arm schedule, commitment preserves the 102.5 mm margin computed from clearance dated at 240 ms. Missing the 278 ms admission cutoff and delaying stop dispatch by 200 ms would delay physical stop onset that far past commitment. Cruise at the TCP limit would add 200 mm before the same stop and breach the 300 mm clearance by 97.5 mm. A later stop must still match its actual entry state; this counterfactual holds cruise speed constant only to show the lost clearance. An arm that waits that long may already occupy a state from which no admissible control avoids the obstacle.

11.5.3 Inevitable collision states

An arm or base that is still clear of every obstacle at the current millisecond can already be doomed to hit one if its speed and remaining distance leave no control input inside its drive and traction limits that can bring it to rest in time. Classical path planning misses this trap when it treats obstacle avoidance as a static geometric query that asks only whether the robot’s configuration overlaps an obstacle volume at the current instant. Because a mobile manipulator moving with momentum and finite braking torque cannot halt instantaneously, its dynamic state (position, velocity, and acceleration, under its traction limits) can cross a boundary beyond which every admissible control trajectory leads to collision long before geometric contact occurs. Such states are inevitable collision states (ICS)10 (Fraichard and Asama 2004).

10 Inevitable collision states and viability: Within the free states, the complement of the ICS set is what Aubin (1991) calls the viability kernel, the states from which some admissible control keeps the system inside its constraints forever. Planning must therefore keep the commitment state inside that kernel, not merely outside the obstacle, and against static obstacles a verified stop to rest is the witness that it is there.

Aubin, Jean-Pierre. 1991. Viability Theory. Systems & Control: Foundations & Applications. Birkhäuser.
Definition 11.4: Inevitable collision state

Inevitable collision state (ICS) is any dynamic plant state \(\MechanicalPlantState(\TimeInstant) \in \PlantStateSpace\) from which every physically admissible future control trajectory \(\FutureControlTrajectory \in \AdmissibleControlSet\) inevitably produces collision with an obstacle or constraint boundary: \[\MechanicalPlantState \in \text{ICS}(\ObstacleStateSet) \iff \forall \ControlInput(\cdot) \in \AdmissibleControlSet, \; \exists \FutureTimeInstant \ge \TimeInstant \text{ such that } \MechanicalPlantState(\FutureTimeInstant) \in \ObstacleStateSet\] where \(\AdmissibleControlSet\) denotes the admissible control input set bounded by actuator torque, traction, and deceleration limits.

  1. Significance: Sets the commitment deadline \(\CommitmentInstant\) in receding-horizon planning; continuing nominal motion past it without an admitted successor can carry the plant into an ICS, and no later replanning can bring it back out.
  2. Distinction: Unlike static geometric collision checks that evaluate only instantaneous spatial overlap (\(\MechanicalPlantState \in \ObstacleStateSet\)), an ICS identifies dynamically trapped states where current momentum and finite braking torque make future boundary breach physically unavoidable.
  3. Common pitfall: Delaying emergency braking in the expectation that an overdue neural planner will complete an evasive path, ignoring that moving mass enters an ICS long before geometric contact occurs.

The commitment deadline \(\CommitmentInstant\) and the admission-checked stopping suffix are co-designed precisely so that the machine initiates braking before its dynamic state enters an inevitable collision state, \(\text{ICS}(\ObstacleStateSet)\). If an action chunk’s stopping suffix requires distance \(\StoppingDistance > \ClearDistance\), that suffix cannot justify admitting the chunk. Failure of this particular stop does not establish that every admissible maneuver collides, as the definition of an inevitable collision state requires. The architecture nevertheless refuses the proposal because it lacks the independently executable stop on which its permission depends. The safety microcontroller therefore enforces the hard admission invariant: \[\StoppingDistance(\CommitmentInstant) \le \ClearDistance(\CommitmentInstant) \tag{18}\] If the controller delays braking past commitment in the hope that an overdue neural replanner will deliver an evasive path, physical momentum can carry the machine into an ICS from which no admissible control input can prevent impact. The commitment deadline is the deadline form of this boundary, fixed at planning time. Its set form, the recoverable set of plant states from which the resident stop still succeeds, is the stopping envelope that Stopping Envelopes develops for the runtime check.

These deadlines and the stop they protect are fixed at admission, before motion begins. The candidate declares payload, contact, effort, and obstacle assumptions. The MCU first validates its fixed header and lineage. It then checks the complete bounded trajectory and suffix, including the seam \((\JointPosition,\JointVelocity,\JointAcceleration)\) against the reachable state envelope from its current estimate, interior extrema, clearance, stopping distance, and a feasible response from every state reachable through physical stop onset. A scan over \(\TrajectorySampleCount\) setpoints is \(\mathcal{O}(\TrajectorySampleCount)\), even with a fixed maximum \(\TrajectorySampleCount\) and measured worst-case execution time; after admission, a distinct \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) per-tick check reads the current state and local reference. A replacement received after the blend deadline passes the same check with a compressed bridge, whose higher acceleration and torque its extrema must still clear. If the check cannot finish before the earlier admission cutoff, the MCU retains the current record only while its own lease, evidence, and fallback remain valid. A candidate received after commitment cannot restore the spent runway.

The planner proposes the suffix. The MCU latches the validated payload and stop profile into protected memory before enabling TRACK, and it dispatches that resident path before commitment without reading host DRAM. A host crash, bus stall, or corrupted shared packet therefore cannot erase the stop. If the measured state falls outside the latched suffix’s entry region, the MCU uses the state-matched fallback. Torque disable or a spring brake is not a universal substitute for a controlled stop, especially with a suspended load (The Fallback Ladder).

11.5.4 Base stopping budget under jerk-limited deceleration

On the mobile base approaching the rack end at its 1.5 m/s drive limit, the loaded tote rack tolerates the machine’s credible 2 m/s² deceleration only when that deceleration is ramped gradually rather than applied as an abrupt step. The base’s stop is therefore the S-curve of equation 17 with \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) at 2 m/s² and a jerk bound of 7.11 m/s³. That jerk bound is the peak jerk of the polynomial stop from the 1.5 m/s drive limit and the stop jerk bound already listed on the machine card (The running machine's card). Under it, every speed above 0.5625 m/s reaches the deceleration plateau. At the drive limit, this suffix lasts 1,031.25 ms and travels 773.44 mm, compared with 562.5 mm for constant-deceleration braking in equation 2.2 and 843.75 mm for the polynomial with the same peaks. Because the jerk ramps cost the equivalent of 140.6 ms of cruise, the suffix adds 210.94 mm to the budget that The latency waterfall left at 912.9 mm. This raises the running total to 1,123.84 mm and exceeds the 1.10 m rack-end clear distance by 23.84 mm. Because this partial total still omits the unqualified dispatch-to-admission interval, which can only add travel, the 23.84 mm overrun is a conditional design deficit rather than a complete measured response bound.

Plotted as velocity against time, the area under each curve is its stopping distance, and the jerk ramps appear as the rounded start and finish that stretch the S-curve and the polynomial past the constant-deceleration line (figure 6). Stacked onto the budget’s other terms, only the constant-deceleration stop, which no drive can execute, ends inside the 1.10 m rack-end clearance.

Even the shortest stop under the stipulated bounds leaves a 23.84 mm deficit at the 1.5 m/s drive limit, so the robot cannot cruise at that limit in this aisle under the stated deceleration and jerk bounds. The deficit is narrow and it rests on a stipulated jerk bound, so a measured jerk tolerance could move it in either direction. The machine must either derate its operating speed or detect the boundary with more usable clearance ahead; a longer prediction or evidence lifetime alone creates no stopping room. This is the first law’s stopping condition (principle 1) with one more term, because a stop the load can tolerate is a longer stop and the distance it adds comes out of the same clear distance. This calculation concerns the fixed rack-end boundary. A person entering the aisle requires separately qualified moving-actor bounds and crossing control or an operating restriction; this static clearance calculation does not establish that protection. Under the selected body and aisle constraints, reducing speed can recover clearance. Stopping Envelopes adds the tracking allowance and derives a candidate speed; permission to operate still requires complete capture-to-admission timing, positive stopping reserve, and qualification under the declared conditions.

↩︎ The intrusion distance an approaching pedestrian adds to the stopping budget, and the site controls it demands, are established in Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay.

For either body, the admission and fallback decisions in table 2 are distinct from an emergency power cut. The mug reach and the aisle approach now have a complete execution argument: a timed prefix, a checked seam, and a stop reserved under stated conditions. With those obligations fixed, trajectory generators can be compared by the candidate work they perform rather than by treating their outputs as permission.

Checkpoint 11.3: Commitment and full stopping clearance

Before committing an autonomous machine to an action chunk, verify your understanding of commitment deadlines, stopping budgets, and trajectory continuity:

11.6 Choosing a Trajectory Generator

When a mobile manipulator reaches around a conveyor guard, a planner that searches configuration space can run out of time before finding a route. A local trajectory optimizer can get trapped in a non-convex pocket behind the guard. A learned action-chunk policy can propose a smooth reach that clips the guard or exceeds a joint’s torque limit. The three algorithmic families that propose motion, geometric search, continuous optimization, and learned action chunking, each fail in a different way if treated as self-certifying. In all three paradigms, deciding where the body can travel geometrically remains separate from determining how quickly actuators can safely traverse that path. Regardless of which family generates a proposal, the resulting trajectory remains an unverified candidate that requires exhaustive dynamic validation before the execution engine commits it to the hardware.

The conveyor reach also fixes what each family can afford. A replacement must be admitted every 50 ms, and 18 ms of every replacement’s dispatch-to-admission allocation is already reserved for whole-plan validation, while a chunk policy spends another 40 ms of it on inference. To sustain the intended renewal cadence, the generation pipeline must provide both the allocated latency and the throughput that overlapping requests require. A solver without a bounded completion time needs a timeout that leaves the resident fallback executable; it can also run outside the renewal loop, planning the route around the guard and handing the loop a corridor to follow. That deadline, more than the quality of the motion, sorts the three families below.

Sampling-based geometric planners explore configuration space without requiring an explicit algebraic representation of obstacles. Probabilistic Roadmaps (PRM) (Kavraki et al. 1996) construct multi-query roadmaps by sampling collision-free configurations and connecting nearby neighbors with local collision-free segments, amortizing graph construction costs across multiple tasks. Conversely, Rapidly-exploring Random Trees (RRT) (LaValle 1998) target single-query problems by growing a space-filling tree from the initial configuration toward unexplored territory. Its asymptotically optimal counterpart, RRT* (Karaman and Frazzoli 2011), continuously rewires parent connections within local neighborhoods as samples accumulate, ensuring that the discovered path cost converges almost surely to the theoretical optimum (Karaman and Frazzoli 2011). These methods resolve geometric routes through complex obstacle geometry, yet their completeness guarantees remain strictly probabilistic: the probability of discovering a feasible path approaches one only as the number of samples approaches infinity. When a sampling planner exhausts its computation budget without finding a path, its timeout provides no proof that the route is obstructed. The admission logic must therefore treat planner silence as an unresolved proposal rather than a verified safety determination. Furthermore, evaluating configuration samples and connecting edges requires repeated collision queries against environment geometry, usually accelerated by enclosing the manipulator links in bounding spheres or capsules, and these queries dominate the planner’s run time. Even when successful, search yields only an un-timed geometric path with discontinuous derivative transitions. The system must still apply time parameterization, smooth corner blends, joint torque verification, and a valid stopping continuation before dispatching the trajectory to physical actuators.

Schulman, John, Yan Duan, Jonathan Ho, Alex Lee, Ibrahim Awwal, Henry Bradlow, Jia Pan, Sachin Patil, Ken Goldberg, and Pieter Abbeel. 2014. “Motion Planning with Sequential Convex Optimization and Convex Collision Checking.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 33 (9): 1251–70. https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364914528132.

The complementary classical formulation is constrained continuous trajectory optimization, exemplified by Covariant Hamiltonian Optimization for Motion Planning (CHOMP) (Ratliff et al. 2009), TrajOpt (Schulman et al. 2014), and receding-horizon Model Predictive Control (MPC) (Tedrake 2023; Lynch and Park 2017). Rather than evaluating discrete graph nodes, continuous trajectory optimization represents motion as a continuous, time-indexed curve \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)\) and minimizes an objective functional that balances dynamic smoothness against obstacle proximity:

\[\min_{\GeneralizedCoordinates(t)} \quad \TrajectoryOptimizationObjective[\GeneralizedCoordinates] = \int_0^\Time \left( \frac{1}{2} \|\dddot{\GeneralizedCoordinates}(\TimeInstant)\|^2 + \ObstacleCostWeight \ObstacleProximityCost(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant)) \right) d\TimeInstant\]

subject to physical boundary conditions and actuator invariants: \[\GeneralizedCoordinates(0) = \GeneralizedCoordinates_0, \quad \GeneralizedVelocities(0) = \GeneralizedVelocities_0, \quad \GeneralizedAccelerations(0) = \GeneralizedAccelerations_0\] \[\GeneralizedCoordinates(\Time) = \GoalConfiguration, \quad \GeneralizedVelocities(\Time) = \ZeroVector, \quad \GeneralizedAccelerations(\Time) = \ZeroVector\] \[|\GeneralizedVelocities(\TimeInstant)| \le \JointVelocityLimit, \quad |\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)| \le \JointTorqueUpperLimit\]

While CHOMP performs functional gradient descent and TrajOpt solves sequential convex subproblems using signed-distance collision constraints, both rely on local optimization. Consequently, non-convex obstacle geometry can trap the solver in infeasible local minima or geometric dead ends. Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS) resolve this susceptibility when the collision-free configuration space can be partitioned beforehand into convex regions. Finding the global sequence of regions and optimizing the polynomial spline within them then formulates as a mixed-integer convex program whose convex relaxation can be solved efficiently (Marcucci et al. 2023). Because generating these convex decompositions requires substantial offline computation, GCS suits static structures such as the conveyor guard far better than dynamic, rapidly shifting environments. Although geometric search or learned policies can propose alternative routes around unforeseen obstructions, any candidate motion must still undergo explicit verification against dynamic torque limits and actuator constraints.

Marcucci, Tobia, Mark Petersen, David von Wrangel, and Russ Tedrake. 2023. “Motion Planning Around Obstacles with Convex Optimization.” Science Robotics 8 (84): eadf7843. https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.adf7843.

11 Model predictive control: MPC began in the process industries of the 1970s, in refineries and chemical plants whose slow dynamics left seconds to minutes for each solve; Qin and Badgwell (2003) trace its industrial lineage from the first IDCOM and dynamic matrix control installations. Moving the same receding-horizon loop onto an arm tracked at 1 kHz shrinks that time to milliseconds, which is why a solver’s deadline, and not its optimum, decides what reaches admission.

Qin, S. Joe, and Thomas A. Badgwell. 2003. “A Survey of Industrial Model Predictive Control Technology.” Control Engineering Practice 11 (7): 733–64. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0967-0661(02)00186-7.
Tedrake, Russ. 2023. Robotic Manipulation: Perception, Planning, and Control. Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

Repeatedly re-optimizing from an updated state estimate forms the operational foundation of classical Model Predictive Control (MPC)11. In this receding-horizon paradigm, an optimizer computes an optimal trajectory over a finite horizon \(\Time\), dispatches an initial prefix to the actuator controllers, and re-solves the problem from the newly observed state on the subsequent cycle. The objective functional ranks candidate motions by performance, while hard constraints eliminate trajectories that exceed physical limits. Classical discrete-time MPC frames this trade-off as a constrained numerical optimization problem over steps \(\HorizonStepIndex = 0, \dots, \MpcPredictionHorizon-1\) (Tedrake 2023; Lynch and Park 2017):

\[\begin{aligned} \min_{\MpcControlSequence} \quad & \sum_{k=0}^{\MpcPredictionHorizon-1} \ell(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k) + \MpcTerminalCost(\TerminalPlantState) \\ \text{subject to} \quad & \MechanicalPlantState_{k+1} = \DiscretePlantDynamics(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k), \quad \HorizonStepIndex = 0, \dots, \MpcPredictionHorizon-1 \\ & \MechanicalPlantState_k \in \SafeStateSet, \quad \ControlInput_k \in \AdmissibleControlSet, \quad \HorizonStepIndex = 0, \dots, \MpcPredictionHorizon-1 \\ & \TerminalPlantState \in \TerminalInvariantSet \end{aligned} \tag{19}\]

The variables in equation 19 decouple the predicted state sequence \(\MechanicalPlantState_k\) from the selected control inputs \(\ControlInput_k\). The dynamic transition map \(\DiscretePlantDynamics(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k)\) propagates the system state forward, requiring every intermediate step to remain admissible. The stage cost \(\ell(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k)\) penalizes tracking error and control effort, while the safe state set \(\SafeStateSet\) enforces collision margins derived from the spatial perception pipeline. The admissible control set \(\AdmissibleControlSet\) bounds actuator torques and voltages according to verified hardware limits.

The terminal constraint forces the horizon endpoint into a terminal invariant set, \(\TerminalPlantState \in \TerminalInvariantSet\). In classical control theory, recursive feasibility guarantees that if a feasible solution exists at control cycle \(\ControlCycleIndex\), a feasible solution remains provably discoverable at cycle \(\ControlCycleIndex+1\). Establishing this property requires an accurate dynamic model and a terminal set \(\TerminalInvariantSet\) within which an invariant fallback control law maintains safety indefinitely. In an embodied robotic system, this terminal invariant is implemented through a validated braking continuation that brings the machine to rest within verified spatial clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)). Bringing the machine to a standstill does not guarantee indefinite safety on its own, because dynamic obstacles can move into the workspace of a stationary robot unless external interlocks preserve the boundary.

Continuous-time optimal control problems are typically transcribed into finite-dimensional nonlinear programs through direct shooting or direct collocation.12 Direct shooting forward-integrates the control trajectory \(\ControlInput(\TimeInstant)\), yielding a compact optimization problem that can nonetheless become ill-conditioned over long horizons. Direct collocation parameterizes both states and controls using piecewise polynomial splines, enforcing dynamic defect constraints \(\PlantDynamicsFunction(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k) - \PlantStateRate_k = \ZeroVector\) at interior knots to generate large, sparse nonlinear programs that converge rapidly near smooth trajectories (Hargraves and Paris 1987). However, theoretical MPC guarantees hold only when the dynamic model is accurate, constraints are satisfied, and the numerical solver finishes before its real-time deadline. Contact transitions, stick-slip friction, and unexpected loads degrade solver convergence, often causing iterations to time out. Learned action chunking eliminates online numerical solves during inference, but neural policies do not enforce the terminal invariant constraint \(\TerminalPlantState \in \TerminalInvariantSet\). An unvalidated action chunk can easily end at high velocity where the robot lacks adequate braking room.

12 Direct collocation defects in real-time solvers: Direct collocation, introduced for trajectory optimization by Hargraves and Paris (1987), turns a continuous-time control problem into a finite nonlinear program by requiring the defect \(\CollocationDefect_k = \PlantStateRate(\TimeInstant_k) - \PlantDynamicsFunction(\MechanicalPlantState_k, \ControlInput_k) = \ZeroVector\) at each collocation point. Contact transitions and unmodeled friction make the problem non-smooth, and the solver then loses its fast local convergence and needs more iterations than a fixed control deadline may allow.

Hargraves, Charles R., and Stephen W. Paris. 1987. “Direct Trajectory Optimization Using Nonlinear Programming and Collocation.” Journal of Guidance, Control, and Dynamics 10 (4): 338–42. https://doi.org/10.2514/3.20223.

Decoupling generation into a learned proposer and a constrained optimizer reconciles statistical representation with physical safety. The learned policy acts as a proposer, generating behavioral candidates conditioned on raw visual tokens and task semantics. A constrained trajectory generator then adjusts the proposed trajectory to satisfy boundary conditions, actuator limits, and spatial clearances. Because this numerical projection can become infeasible or miss its real-time deadline, it operates entirely on the untrusted proposal side of the architecture, upstream of independent safety admission.

Mathematically, this dual-stage architecture formulates trajectory generation as a constrained projection operator \(\FeasibleTrajectoryProjection(\LearnedActionProposal)\):

\[\GeneralizedCoordinates^*(\TimeInstant) = \FeasibleTrajectoryProjection(\LearnedActionProposal) = \arg\min_{\GeneralizedCoordinates(t)} \int_0^\Time \|\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant) - \LearnedNominalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\|_{\StateTrackingWeightMatrix}^2 d\TimeInstant + \int_0^\Time \|\dddot{\GeneralizedCoordinates}(\TimeInstant)\|_{\JerkWeightMatrix}^2 d\TimeInstant \tag{20}\]

The projection begins at the planned activation seam, so its initial-state constraints refer to that seam rather than to the state measured when the planning request begins. Upstream synthesis uses the projected reference and its reachable-state envelope; at activation, the MCU instantiates and checks the bounded bridge against measured state. The candidate remains subject to physical boundary conditions and safety invariants across the execution interval \(\TimeInstant \in [0, \Time]\): \[\GeneralizedCoordinates(0) = \SeamActualConfiguration, \quad \GeneralizedVelocities(0) = \SeamActualVelocities, \quad \GeneralizedAccelerations(0) = \SeamActualAccelerations\] \[\GeneralizedCoordinates(\TimeInstant) \in \FreeConfigurationSpace, \quad |\GeneralizedVelocities(\TimeInstant)| \le \JointVelocityLimit, \quad |\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)| \le \JointTorqueUpperLimit\] \[\StoppingDistance(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\CommitmentInstant), \GeneralizedVelocities(\CommitmentInstant)) \le \ClearDistance(\CommitmentInstant)\]

In equation 20, the primary term penalizes tracking deviation from the learned nominal trajectory \(\LearnedNominalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\) using positive semidefinite state weights \(\StateTrackingWeightMatrix\), while the regularization term penalizes joint jerk \(\|\dddot{\GeneralizedCoordinates}(\TimeInstant)\|_{\JerkWeightMatrix}^2\) to minimize mechanical vibration and wear. The weighted quadratic norm is defined as \(\|\WeightedNormArgument\|_{\NormWeightMatrix}^2 = \WeightedNormArgument^\top \NormWeightMatrix \WeightedNormArgument\), and \(\FeasibleTrajectorySet\) denotes the feasible set of trajectories satisfying the displayed physical constraints. These constraints enforce the dynamic invariants that unconstrained learned models cannot guarantee: \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) continuity with the currently executing state, collision avoidance within the free configuration space \(\FreeConfigurationSpace\), kinematic joint speed bounds, full rigid-body inverse dynamics torque limits, and certified stopping viability at the commitment horizon \(\CommitmentInstant\). Even when the optimizer finds a feasible trajectory, that solution remains contingent on model assumptions regarding payload mass and obstacle positions; the independent admission gate must verify those assumptions before granting hardware permission.

Altering the proposer modifies the computational budget and latency profile, but leaves the physical safety obligations unchanged. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) (Zhao et al. 2023) infers an entire chunk of future joint setpoints in a single forward pass and smooths replanning boundaries by ensembling overlapping temporal chunks. However, simple coordinate averaging across predictions fails to enforce higher-order boundary derivatives, which can induce severe acceleration transients at chunk transitions. Diffusion policies (Chi et al. 2025) capture complex, multimodal behavioral distributions through iterative score-based denoising, but the multiple neural network evaluations required during inference add substantial latency to the replacement planning path. Alternatively, a neural model can warm-start a numerical trajectory optimizer by providing initial collocation knots. While a good warm-start drastically reduces sequential quadratic programming iterations, an out-of-distribution observation can seed the optimizer in an infeasible basin of attraction, causing solver divergence and missed execution deadlines. Generator selection therefore balances configuration-space exploration, constrained local tracking, and multimodal perceptual synthesis against the strict temporal deadlines required to validate and admit candidate trajectories. Table 3 compares the inputs, constraints, and seam obligations of these synthesis methods. Its sampling-based MPC row, model predictive path integral control (MPPI), rolls many sampled control sequences through the dynamics model and returns their cost-weighted average. MPPI reappears as a base controller in section 11.6.1.

The mechanisms in this chapter are durable; the systems that implement them change quickly. Table 4 records the named systems a practitioner meets today, each tied to the mechanism it implements. Its final row shifts from the seven-joint arm to the wheeled mobile base, whose rolling wheels constrain the velocity space directly.

11.6.1 Mobile-base planning under nonholonomic constraints

Unlike the seven-joint arm, the mobile base cannot follow a straight line interpolated between two poses in the warehouse aisle, because its two coaxial drive wheels roll without slipping and therefore cannot slide sideways along their axle. Expressed in terms of heading angle \(\HeadingAngle\), forward linear speed \(\BaseForwardSpeed\), and yaw rate \(\YawRate\), the differential-drive (unicycle) kinematics satisfy

\[\BaseAbscissaVelocity = \BaseForwardSpeed\cos\HeadingAngle, \qquad \BaseOrdinateVelocity = \BaseForwardSpeed\sin\HeadingAngle, \qquad \BaseHeadingRate = \YawRate, \qquad \BaseAbscissaVelocity\sin\HeadingAngle - \BaseOrdinateVelocity\cos\HeadingAngle = 0 \tag{21}\]

The non-integrable relation in equation 21 forms a nonholonomic constraint,13 restricting the instantaneous velocity space without diminishing the dimension of the reachable configuration space \(\PlanarSpecialEuclideanGroup\) (Siciliano et al. 2009). The base can reach any planar pose in the aisle, but not with any velocity. It can drive forward at \(\BaseForwardSpeed\) and turn at \(\YawRate\) about the center of its axle, while the no-slip condition forbids translation along the axle (figure 7). A pure sideways offset is therefore reached as a maneuver rather than a step: turn in place, drive along the new heading, and turn back. A base planner must therefore search directly over kinematically admissible control trajectories rather than straight geometric paths in configuration space. Furthermore, each wheel’s tangential speed equals \(\BaseForwardSpeed \pm \YawRate \WheelTrackWidth/2\) for track width \(\WheelTrackWidth\), so hardware motor limits constrain linear velocity and turning rate jointly.

13 Holonomic (Greek holos, whole, and nomos, law): Hertz (1894) introduced the term for systems whose constraints obey integral laws, relations among positions alone, in contrast to material systems in general, which obey only differential laws. A wheel’s no-slip rule is a differential law that cannot be integrated into such a relation. The distinction decides the planner’s search space, since a holonomic arm can be planned as a path through configuration space while the base must be planned through its controls.

Hertz, Heinrich. 1894. Die Prinzipien Der Mechanik in Neuem Zusammenhange Dargestellt. Vol. 3. Gesammelte Werke. Johann Ambrosius Barth.
Siciliano, Bruno, Lorenzo Sciavicco, Luigi Villani, and Giuseppe Oriolo. 2009. Robotics: Modelling, Planning and Control. Springer Science & Business Media.

Local base planners enforce nonholonomic constraints by planning directly in velocity space over a receding time horizon. The dynamic window approach (DWA) (Fox et al. 1997) maps each control pair \((\BaseForwardSpeed, \YawRate)\) to a circular trajectory arc and evaluates candidate pairs through two nested dynamic criteria. Reachable velocities comprise pairs achievable within actuator acceleration limits over the replanning window. With the mobile manipulator’s base acceleration limit of 2 m/s² and a 50 ms chunk renewal interval, this dynamic window spans 0.1 m/s on either side of the current forward velocity. Admissible velocities select the subset from which the base can decelerate to a complete stop before reaching the nearest obstacle along that arc, satisfying \(\BaseForwardSpeed \le \sqrt{2\,\ArcObstacleDistance(\BaseForwardSpeed,\YawRate)\,\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}\). From these admissible candidates, DWA selects the control pair maximizing an objective that balances target heading alignment, obstacle clearance, and forward speed. MPPI (Williams et al. 2017), the sampling-based MPC of table 3, replaces this deterministic velocity grid by rolling forward thousands of stochastic control sequences through the dynamic model on a GPU, synthesizing an optimal control action as a cost-weighted trajectory distribution.
