Production navigation frameworks such as ROS 2 Nav2 incorporate both controller families (Macenski et al. 2023).

Fox, Dieter, Wolfram Burgard, and Sebastian Thrun. 1997. “The Dynamic Window Approach to Collision Avoidance.” IEEE Robotics & Automation Magazine 4 (1): 23–33. https://doi.org/10.1109/100.580977.
Williams, Grady, Nolan Wagener, Brian Goldfain, Paul Drews, James M. Rehg, Byron Boots, and Evangelos A. Theodorou. 2017. “Information Theoretic MPC for Model-Based Reinforcement Learning.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 1714–21. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2017.7989202.
Macenski, Steve, Tom Moore, David V. Lu, Alexey Merzlyakov, and Michael Ferguson. 2023. “From the Desks of ROS Maintainers: A Survey of Modern & Capable Mobile Robotics Algorithms in the Robot Operating System 2.” Robotics and Autonomous Systems 168: 104493. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.robot.2023.104493.

The admissibility test applies the core stopping requirement across candidate arcs, yet its classical textbook formulation assumes instantaneous, constant-deceleration braking. On a straight trajectory with 1.10 m of rack-end clearance, this simplified test admits forward speeds up to 2.10 m/s, well above the physical drive limit of 1.5 m/s. However, when evaluated against the conditional stopping subtotal derived in section 11.5.4 (which accounts for sensor latency, communication delays, localization uncertainty margins, and jerk-limited deceleration), that same clearance fails to guarantee safety even at the nominal drive limit. A mobile-base trajectory generator, whether a classical DWA planner or a learned chunk policy, is therefore a proposer of candidate velocities. The real-time permission path must also charge dispatch-to-admission travel before validating each proposed trajectory against the full stopping budget.

The choice of trajectory generator governs how candidate motions are discovered and determines the computational burden placed on downstream verification. However, the system’s safety enforcement mechanisms must respond consistently regardless of how a proposal was generated. In particular, the execution architecture must cleanly differentiate between rejecting an invalid replacement candidate and responding to unexpected environmental changes. Rejecting an unvalidated proposal leaves the active trajectory in control only while its state, evidence, lease, and stopping reserve remain valid, whereas detecting an unexpected obstacle incursion along the executed path demands an immediate transition to verified braking.

11.7 Trajectory Planning Failure Modes and Infeasible Seams

On the mug reach, a replacement trajectory can fail in three distinct regimes: within the candidate itself, along the boundary where it joins the executing motion, or during execution when the environment changes. The point where a failure first manifests dictates which subsystem has the sensory evidence to reject the trajectory, as well as how much time and clearance remain to bring the machine to a safe stop. Pre-execution candidate and boundary checks intercept the first two failure modes before setpoints can reach actuator registers, whereas runtime monitoring guards against the third.

11.7.1 Checking limits and boundaries before execution

If an unvalidated chunk reaches actuator registers directly, a single out-of-bounds waypoint or an unblended velocity step at the splice instant will fault the drive before feedback monitoring can react. Before any proposed trajectory setpoint can influence actuator registers, a deterministic safety MCU therefore screens the candidate record through two independent admission gates within a measured worst-case execution budget. Fixed-degree seam calculations require bounded compute per axis, whereas checking a complete setpoint sequence scales with the candidate horizon length:

  1. Pre-execution Bound Checker: Evaluates the proposed neural action chunk in isolation across the full candidate horizon \([\TrackingStartInstant, \NominalPlanExpiry]\) against intrinsic kinematic, dynamic, and stopping invariants. The checker evaluates margin functions \(\LimitMarginFunction(\TimeInstant) = \CheckedSignalLimit - |\CheckedSignal(\TimeInstant)|\) across the signals it checks. Joint positions \(\JointPosition(\TimeInstant)\) are checked against their limits. Joint velocities are checked against contracted limits \(|\JointVelocity(\TimeInstant)| \le \SpeedBound - \frac{1}{2} \ProfileAccelerationBound \SamplingPeriod\) (equation 10, or the \(\frac{1}{4}\) reserve under the equal-velocity, zero-acceleration endpoint conditions and jerk bound of section 11.4.2). Free-space inverse-dynamics joint torques are sampled with a bound on their inter-sample variation and checked against the rated torque, \(|\JointTorque(\TimeInstant)| = |\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)| \le \RatedJointTorque\). The checker also enforces the binding stopping invariant: the candidate’s matched stopping suffix must satisfy \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) throughout observed free space. If any knot point or interior interval yields \(\LimitMarginFunction(\TimeInstant) < 0\) or causes the stopping runway to breach available clearance, the gate immediately rejects the candidate and logs a diagnostic fault record containing the joint index, timestamp, and negative margin. Gate rejection causes neither mechanical shudder nor physical pause, because the low-level tracking controller continues along the previously admitted trajectory as long as that trajectory’s state, sensor evidence, lease, and stopping reserve remain valid. A rejected replacement simply fails to renew that lease.
  2. Boundary Checker: Evaluates the transition seam between the currently executing trajectory \(\Glyph{A}\) and the candidate replacement \(\Glyph{B}\) at transition time \(\SeamInstant\). Because unconstrained sequence decoders predict open-loop action tokens without terminal derivative matching, concatenating raw chunks frequently produces sharp acceleration steps. The boundary checker addresses this in two stages. During early admission, it screens the reachable seam-state envelope projected from trajectory \(\Glyph{A}\). At activation, it incorporates real-time encoder estimates \((\MeasuredSeamConfiguration, \MeasuredSeamVelocities, \MeasuredSeamAccelerations)\) and their error bounds to calculate the physical seam gap \((\FiniteChange\GeneralizedCoordinates, \FiniteChange\GeneralizedVelocities, \FiniteChange\GeneralizedAccelerations)\). It then evaluates whether an analytical \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) quintic bridge (equation 12) can reconcile the boundary without violating torque limits or the motor drive’s voltage and current-slew constraints. A fast one-tick estimate, \(\SeamStepTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia \frac{\FiniteChange\JointVelocity}{\SamplingPeriod}\), diagnoses the demand of an unblended one-tick transition. Failure of that estimate cannot reject a finite bridge by itself; admission checks the bridge at its actual duration, with its endpoint states, interior derivatives, torque and electrical bounds, and swept clearance.

For the CSG-25-50 joint in the seam analysis, an idealized calculation accounts solely for its 0.18 kg·m² reflected rotor inertia over a single 1 ms tick and allocates the entire 127 N·m repeated-peak rating purely to acceleration. It yields an idealized ceiling of 0.69 rad/s on that hypothetical one-tick step \(\FiniteChange\JointVelocity\), not on a finite-duration bridge. In practice, the available torque margin is substantially smaller once link and payload inertia, gravity, friction, feedforward torque commands, coil current rise times, and thermal or DC-bus limits are deducted. The admission path must evaluate the finite bridge against this state-dependent torque margin and the drive’s measured step response. The gate nevertheless rejects a step velocity discontinuity outright, even one whose idealized single-tick torque falls below that ceiling, because continuous physical motion requires a smooth, bounded \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) bridge that satisfies its own interior limit checks.14

14 Drive and resonance response: A sharp command can excite a joint’s identified flexible modes, but their frequencies and damping depend on the transmission and load. Current limiting, desaturation detection, DC-bus response, and shutdown depend on the selected driver and its configuration; one vendor’s desaturation-protection design note shows how such trip thresholds are set (Chen and Ramachandran 2025).

Chen, Tilden, and Akshaya Ramachandran. 2025. How TPSI3133 DESAT Can Protect Your Expensive SiC MOSFETs and IGBTs. Application Note No. SLVAG18. Texas Instruments.

11.7.2 Runtime tracking monitors and unexpected contact

↪ The cyclic checking that enforces these trips on every tick, and the independence the monitor needs from the proposer, are developed in Admission, Cyclic Checking, and Monitor Independence.

When the arm reaches past the conveyor guard with 0.020 m of budgeted swept-volume clearance, a slipping payload or an unexpected contact after admission can invalidate the dynamic and geometric assumptions under which the plan was cleared. Independent runtime monitors must detect this divergence while remaining obstacle clearance still allows a controlled stop. They track four primary signals on every tick: joint state divergence, tracking error velocity, unexpected contact wrenches, and violations of the geometric assumptions declared in the trajectory record’s sidecar (section 11.8). This monitoring relies on spatial margin budgeting, in which geometric obstacle clearance is not simply awarded to control tracking error. Mechanical compliance under load, structural deflection, and drivetrain backlash consume physical clearance before any feedback tracking error becomes observable at the encoders. The tracking error allowance \(\TrackingErrorAllowance\) therefore equals the total swept-volume clearance minus the structural deflection and gearbox compliance allowance \(\DeflectionComplianceAllowance\).

On the conveyor-guard reach, once payload deflection and gearbox compliance claim an allowance \(\DeflectionComplianceAllowance\) of 0.005 m, the nominal tracking allowance \(\TrackingErrorAllowance\) drops from 0.020 m to 0.015 m. This remainder represents an upper ceiling on allocatable margin rather than a direct trip threshold, because latencies across detection, dispatch, and actuator response, along with state-estimation uncertainty and the swept motion of the fallback maneuver, all require dedicated clearance reserves. The arm tracker’s validated tracking bound, the arm’s counterpart to the base tracking bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) that The Tracker Handoff and Dynamic Reference Envelopes derives, must fit within these 0.015 m and is checked by the real-time safety loop on every control tick. If the measured Cartesian tool-position error \(\|\MeasuredTrackedPosition(\TimeInstant)-\ReferencePosition(\TimeInstant)\|\) exceeds \(\TrackingErrorBound\), or if a wrist-mounted six-axis force-torque sensor detects an unmodeled 10 N contact during free-space transit, the monitor immediately revokes nominal tracking and commands the validated state-matched fallback. Active physical deceleration begins once the measured detection, communication, and actuator-onset delays elapse.

A trajectory plan can also exhaust its nominal setpoints before a subsequent candidate passes admission. The safety MCU cannot simply allow the execution pointer to overrun the trajectory buffer, nor can it treat a stationary position-hold setpoint as an active, verified stop. Instead, the two deadline tiers of section 11.5.1 end the window of validity on the MCU’s monotonic clock, whichever fires first. Furthermore, computing stopping clearance requires accounting for the full physical chain: sensing and detection delays, dispatch latency, actuator torque rise times, and the swept volume across the deceleration profile. Elementary calculations assuming instantaneous or constant deceleration fail to model the true physical trajectory of a smooth \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stopping suffix.

Every checker and monitor described here depends on an explicit execution contract between the upstream trajectory generator and the downstream real-time controller. If a planner delivers only a raw sequence of Cartesian or joint waypoints, without its validity lease, clearance margins, boundary derivative bounds, and certified stopping suffix, the low-level permission path has no principled way to distinguish safe nominal motion from dangerous dynamic drift. Safe autonomous execution requires that geometry, dynamics, and timing be verified together before setpoints ever reach actuator registers.

11.8 The Trajectory Record

Suppose the planner hands the low-level controller a bare array of 16 joint setpoints for the reach toward the mug, without the obstacle epoch, payload assumption, and pre-checked stopping suffix under which those setpoints were cleared. The MCU then has no way to tell whether the plan is still safe to run when the host stalls or the load changes. The trajectory record, handed over immutably at every replanning cycle, lets the low-level permission path decide whether to admit those setpoints without consulting the high-level planner again. The record carries the header, four deadlines, two deadline tiers, and assumption sidecar defined with the planning handoff in section 11.2.2. It works as a cyber-physical contract rather than a passive message format. Every field encodes a physical invariant that the downstream runtime can verify before a command crosses the causal boundary.

By pairing a staged admission check with the latched stopping profile of section 11.5.3, the record lets the MCU evaluate safety independently of the host planner. Even if the host operating system crashes mid-motion, the resident emergency stop profile remains intact on the real-time controller. Bringing the physical machine to rest still demands operational sensors, DC bus energy, and mechanical braking authority. The belief record and intent lease consumed at the planning handoff remain embedded in the record’s lineage. The completed record binds these physical assumptions directly to the proposed motion before forwarding them to the runtime enforcer, where live sensor feedback governs per-tick tracking permission.

Every field in the contract captures an explicit condition of admission. Kinematic boundary derivatives define how the trajectory must mesh with ongoing motion at the seam. Real-time deadlines dictate exactly when nominal tracking must yield to a safe stop. The accompanying sidecar documents the precise geometric and inertial assumptions under which the validator certified the plan. Instead of taking the planner’s assertion of feasibility on faith, the execution runtime uses the record to hold the motion accountable to physical reality.

Hierarchically, the trajectory record answers to the intent lease. The cryptographic digest binds the record to the exact parent lease whose target and effort ceilings the permission path must check against the proposed motion. The motion itself is stored as discrete setpoint arrays or continuous spline coefficients, parameterized by an explicit sample cadence (a 25 ms timestep \(\SetpointStepInterval\) on the running machine, a 40 Hz stream). The record also carries the nominal trajectory expiration \(\NominalPlanExpiry\) (400 ms for a single chunk) and canonical SI units. Pairing this explicit cadence and unit specification with the reference coordinate frame and evidence timestamp established in section 11.2 allows the joint controller to interpolate setpoints and evaluate higher-order derivatives cleanly, eliminating coordinate-frame ambiguities and any reliance on unsynchronized host clocks.

Physical feasibility is never unconditional; it holds only under the environmental geometry and dynamic loads assumed during planning. The immutable sidecar captures these conditions, recording the obstacle-map epoch, payload mass, surface friction coefficients, kinematic limits, validator software version, certified clearance margins, and the source observation identifiers. A cryptographic digest in the fixed header immutably binds this sidecar to the setpoint payload. Because safety proofs cannot extrapolate beyond validated boundaries, the payload and friction values declared in the sidecar must fall strictly within the target operational design domain (ODD) declared in The Evaluation Record. If live sensor feedback indicates that the robot has drifted outside these operating assumptions, the permission path immediately revokes the TRACK state, recognizing that clearance margins computed for one world state do not hold in another. Diagnostic metadata, such as random solver seeds and neural model weight hashes, belongs in this audit sidecar for offline postmortem analysis, keeping the per-tick servo loop lean and deterministic.

↪ Conditional permission, the per-tick safe-set test that holds a clearance margin valid only for the live state, is developed in Safe Sets as Conditional Permission.

On this machine, the chunk policy generates only the nominal motion profile, which the planning runtime then packages into a self-contained record by appending the seam state, the four operational deadlines, and a matched stopping suffix. Inside the fixed header, lineage fields record a monotonic sequence number, the evidence timestamp \(\EvidenceEpoch\), and the digest of the parent intent lease, while timing fields encode the four deadlines from section 11.2.2. Seam fields then define the reference frame, each joint’s initial state \((\JointPosition,\JointVelocity,\JointAcceleration)\) at handoff, and the representation of an admission-checked stopping profile satisfying \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). Content fields specify the payload format, byte length, and an integrity digest spanning both the setpoint payload and the sidecar.

Both the variable-length setpoint sequence and the immutable assumption sidecar sit immediately outside the fixed header. The MCU verifies their composite digest before running the admission checks described in section 11.5. This dual-digest design decouples integrity from provenance: the payload digest validates contents, while the parent digest anchors lineage. Because the permission path admits a motion proposal based entirely on whether its physical invariants check out rather than on the identity of the process that generated it (Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts), the record deliberately omits cryptographic signatures or origin tags.

↪ Byte layout: Trajectory payload illustrates a record carrying a single polynomial stopping suffix; it does not encode the worked piecewise S-curve.

Instantiated for the warehouse reach toward the mug, the record carries its own deadlines, which are those of the alternate timeline, while the running chunk lease decides how long tracking actually continues. Its parent digest points directly to the mug-acquisition intent lease, its coordinates are expressed in the robot base frame, and every timestamp references the MCU’s monotonic clock. Measured relative to \(\TrackingStartInstant\), the timing fields record the commitment deadline at 300 ms, the nominal trajectory end at 400 ms (derived from 16 setpoints spaced at 25 ms), and the final safe-stop horizon at 575 ms. The 240 ms blend deadline remains a planner scheduling target outside the fixed header. Four candidates can end tracking authority: the admission cutoff at 278 ms, the lease timeout 60 ms after the last admitted chunk, the intent lease expiry, and evidence staleness. On the running machine the lease timeout binds first. The commitment fields then serve as a backstop that acts only under a lease long enough to outlast the cutoff.

The worked seam specifies a 275 ms, jerk-limited \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stopping suffix bounded by peak acceleration 5 m/s² and jerk 66.7 m/s³. It is anchored at the commitment state and brings the arm safely to rest from its 1 m/s TCP velocity limit within 137.5 mm. Serializing and admitting this analytical S-curve requires a specified bounded profile encoding and decoder, bound to the admitted path and stop-entry state, with decoding and validation included in the admission budget (a representation not supplied by the illustrative single-polynomial wire layout). Under the running lease, a lease timeout triggers the state-matched fallback built from the measured state. From zero-acceleration cruise that fallback follows the same S-curve and travels the same distance (section 11.5.2), so the lease path and stop together consume 219.5 mm of the available 300 mm clearance. The 278 ms commitment cutoff never extends the lease. In the sidecar, the planner asserts a payload mass bounded by 3 kg, unobstructed free space leading up to the grasp contact, and operating conditions inside the target ODD. The real-time permission path evaluates all of these upon admission and audits them on every control cycle.

In this concrete trajectory, the temporal ordering strictly satisfies \(\TrackingStartInstant\le \CommitmentInstant<\NominalPlanExpiry<\StopCompletionInstant\), and the MCU enforces these timing boundaries according to the TRACK and STOP transition rules developed in section 11.2. Because safety requires both temporal validity and physical realizability, a well-formed timestamp in the header cannot compensate for an unviable stopping suffix. To make latency and timing jitter auditable, the system logs the timestamps for evidence capture, plan request, validation completion, admission, activation, control transfer, and stop completion in a synchronized time domain, exposing the end-to-end response delay of the entire perception-planning-control pipeline.

Every artifact in this runtime chain, from the observation contract and the belief record through the intent lease to the trajectory record itself, declares its physical dependencies and its expiration deadline. The trajectory record proves only what the planner could verify at plan time: that a collision-free stop remains physically feasible from any state the robot might reach before commitment, given the obstacle scene and dynamic payload asserted in its sidecar. Common design pitfalls often mistake this bounded, conditional guarantee for unconditional safety, overlooking what happens when physical reality deviates from the planner’s model.

11.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A planned trajectory remains physically feasible only as long as its environmental, dynamic, and computational assumptions hold true. In embodied systems, failures rarely stem from minor mathematical errors along the nominal interior of a path. Instead, they emerge at operational and physical seams: when dynamic obstacles intrude into an active swept volume, when concatenated action chunks excite mechanical resonances, when host inference pipelines miss scheduling deadlines, or when controllers attempt to stop faster than actuator dynamics permit. Both the trajectory admission gate and the real-time execution monitor must defend against these five failure modes.

Fallacy: Static admission checks protect a machine against obstacle displacements during active execution.

Static geometry confirms clear space only at admission time; dynamic workspaces demand continuous runtime monitoring. Consider a robotic manipulator reaching toward an assembly target. When the trajectory is first admitted, the swept volume between the end-effector and the target is completely clear. Halfway through execution, however, an operator slides a storage tote into the arm’s path. Although the admission check was mathematically correct when computed, the physical workspace has changed. Static pre-screening guarantees only that a trajectory was safe in the past; it cannot guarantee that the path remains clear as time elapses. Execution monitoring must continuously track obstacle clearances and initiate deceleration while adequate stopping distance remains. Systems architects must explicitly budget sensor bus latency and vision pipeline transit times, reserving enough spatial stopping margin to ensure the machine can halt before an encroaching obstacle breaches its swept volume.

Architect’s countermeasure: Pair high-level spatial screening on the host with low-latency local clearance sensing processed directly by the MCU. Explicitly allocate the sensing refresh rate, bus transit latency, and MCU memory footprint. If an unmodeled obstacle breaches the reserved spatial safety margin \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\), immediately abort nominal tracking and engage the pre-validated, state-matched fallback trajectory.

Pitfall: Checking each action chunk alone without examining the motion the chunks compose.

Individual chunk feasibility does not guarantee smooth motion across seams; stochastic neural chatter can overstress a mechanical transmission. When generative sequence policies (such as diffusion models or action-chunking transformers) are sampled stochastically, successive chunks frequently disagree in direction or mismatch acceleration at their boundary. While every individual chunk satisfies kinematic position and velocity bounds in isolation, concatenating them without boundary constraints induces rapid torque reversals \(\FiniteChange\CommandedJointTorque = \EffectiveJointInertia \FiniteChange\JointAcceleration\). Amplified by the reflected rotor inertia (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)), this high-frequency chatter excites flexible structural modes, accelerates gearbox wear, and causes severe Joule heating (\(\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \PhaseResistance\)) in the stator windings. The admission pipeline must evaluate derivative continuity across every chunk boundary seam, enforce \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) quintic smoothing, and track recent motion history to reject oscillating command sequences before they reach the motor drives.

Architect’s countermeasure: Enforce multi-chunk frequency and reversal monitoring across a sliding execution window. Reject any trajectory candidate whose frequency spectrum or repeated effort exceeds the motor’s identified mechanical or thermal envelope. If smoothing filters alter a candidate chunk, re-run the full kinematic, seam continuity, and terminal stop checks on the modified trajectory prior to admission.

Pitfall: Delaying fallback transition past the hard stopping horizon in the hope that delayed compute finishes.

An overdue compute cycle never justifies extending a physical commitment deadline. In an alternate schedule operating with an admission cutoff of 278 ms, the active trajectory record supplies nominal setpoints that expire at 400 ms. The record carries a 275 ms matched stopping suffix that must finish deceleration by 575 ms. To guarantee physical braking onset by 300 ms, the MCU must dispatch the fallback trajectory no later than the 278 ms cutoff unless a verified replacement plan has already arrived. If the host application processor reports that neural inference is nearly complete but has not yet delivered a validated plan, delaying the fallback transition consumes the runway reserved for stopping. The MCU must dispatch the resident stopping suffix by the earlier cutoff so its physical onset occurs at commitment. Progress reports cannot extend a mechanical stopping budget; only an admitted, physically verifiable trajectory justifies continued forward motion.

Architect’s countermeasure: Bind the physical commitment deadline \(\CommitmentInstant\) strictly to a monotonic hardware timer on the MCU. When the timer reaches the admission cutoff, close the admission window and dispatch the resident stopping suffix immediately, disregarding any speculative progress updates from the host processor.

Pitfall: Relying solely on feedforward dynamic models when physical contact or payload mass can change abruptly.

Feedforward model inversion without closed-loop disturbance bounds can drive an unloaded arm beyond its admitted trajectory. Consider an arm lifting a heavy workpiece near its 3 kg payload limit. To track the aggressive trajectory, the controller injects feedforward torque computed from an inverse dynamics model of the fully loaded arm. If the workpiece slips from the gripper mid-lift, the drives continue applying the large lifting torque intended for the heavy load to an arm that is now empty. Without a timely feedback correction or torque clamp, the excess torque accelerates the arm above its planned rate. While the admitted trajectory has not changed, the physical mass properties that justified those torque commands have vanished. High-bandwidth feedback monitoring must detect the resulting tracking error and clamp torque commands before the arm strikes a mechanical joint stop or triggers inverter thermal derating. Feedforward dynamic models significantly reduce tracking lag under nominal conditions, but they must never run without closed-loop supervisory bounds.

Architect’s countermeasure: Implement real-time torque limiters and tracking-error monitoring (\(\|\MeasuredTrackedPosition - \ReferencePosition\| \le \TrackingErrorBound\)) directly inside the 1 kHz servo loop. If an abrupt payload drop or unexpected contact causes acceleration to spike, clamp motor currents immediately and transition the system to compliance mode or an emergency stop.

Fallacy: A trajectory can omit an achievable terminal stopping suffix if replacement plans are expected to arrive in time.

A trajectory without a pre-latched stopping continuation is an unhedged bet on infinite compute availability. Traveling at its drive limit of 1.5 m/s, a mobile manipulator’s base requires 1,031.25 ms for even the shortest deceleration profile its kinematic bounds permit. That minimum stop follows an S-curve profile \(\Time=\CruiseSpeed/\ProfileAccelerationBound+\ProfileAccelerationBound/\JerkLimit\) evaluated at a credible braking acceleration 2 m/s² and jerk limit 7.11 m/s³. Sizing a proposed stopping suffix using the idealized constant-deceleration formula \(\CruiseSpeed/\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) yields 750 ms. However, no physical actuator can ramp deceleration instantaneously without infinite jerk. Forcing a stop into that truncated duration inevitably violates the acceleration bound, the jerk limit, or both, exceeding what the chassis and its payload can tolerate. Expecting the next plan to arrive on time does not make an unfeasible fallback executable. The admission gate must verify that the terminal stopping suffix strictly respects \(\ProfileAccelerationBound\) and \(\JerkLimit\) throughout its duration. With an S-curve, this requires validating \(\StoppingDuration\) against equation 17 and confirming that the stopping distance satisfies \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) within verified clearance. The admission gate must reject any record that fails this test. Every admitted trajectory requires a physically executable termination path to protect the machine when upstream inference stalls.

Architect’s countermeasure: Require every candidate trajectory record to incorporate a complete, closed-form \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stopping profile along its admitted corridor. Deceleration and jerk along this profile must remain strictly within vehicle and payload stability limits, while the stopping distance must satisfy \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) within verified clear space. Reject any record at the admission gate that lacks this independently executable stopping guarantee.

11.10 Summary

A trajectory transforms a high-level goal into timed physical motion. On an active machine, it must resolve three questions that a geometric path leaves unanswered: how long the motion remains valid and executable, how it splices smoothly into the motion already under way, and how the machine stops safely if no replacement arrives. A multi-step task such as the reach for a mug progresses through a continuous sequence of renewed, validated trajectory prefixes without forcing the entire task into a single observation window. Predictive models and planners generate candidate trajectories, but the runtime system grants execution authority only after verifying fresh sensor evidence, admission deadlines, and the machine’s true state. When a replacement trajectory arrives late, the controller falls back to its resident stopping maneuver. Tail latency bounds (\(\DesignLatencyQuantile(\ReplacementLatency)\)) therefore quantify the expected frequency of service degradation to this safe fallback, while the machine’s physical safety rests on the stopping profile itself.

Whether a physical system can track a candidate trajectory is distinct from whether that trajectory avoids obstacles. Kinematic feasibility alone can fail in subtle ways. Near kinematic singularities, the manipulator Jacobian maps modest Cartesian end-effector velocities into unbounded joint rates. Damped least-squares inversion caps these joint steps, but does so by accepting tracking error that can still push joints against their limits. Dynamic feasibility imposes even stricter constraints. The rigid-body manipulator equations translate planned motions into actuator torques, where reflected rotor inertia amplifies any velocity or acceleration discontinuity across trajectory seams into severe torque spikes. A quintic bridge matches position, velocity, and acceleration across the boundary, eliminating these impulsive torque requests. Similarly, time-optimal path parameterization drives actuators along their torque envelopes, but bang-bang acceleration profiles demand instantaneous torque steps that physical drives cannot achieve. Enforcing an explicit jerk bound rounds these transitions at minimal time cost, turning theoretical path timings into executable motor commands. Whether candidates originate from sampling-based search, trajectory optimization, or learned action chunking, the underlying physical obligations remain unchanged. Each planning method generates a prospective motion proposal, but never a license to execute without validation.

Real-time execution relies on strict timing contracts between the planner and the low-level controller. A 60 ms lease halts a stalled proposal stream using a state-matched stopping response, reserving an illustrative 80.5 mm safety buffer to absorb sensing delay and model uncertainty. In an alternate longer-lease configuration, replacement admission closes strictly by 278 ms so that physical deceleration can initiate no later than 300 ms. Both regimes enforce the identical systems invariant: every active trajectory must reserve sufficient travel to accommodate perception-action latency and the entire stopping maneuver. The base faces the same arithmetic. Its jerk-limited S-curve, the shortest stop under the stipulated load bounds, raises the partial stopping budget at the drive limit to 1,123.84 mm (row 6 of The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget). Because this exceeds the available 1.10 m rack-end clearance, the runtime controller must proactively restrict operating speed. Table 5 lists the equations behind these admission decisions, detailing the physical limit each bounds and where it is derived in this chapter.

Key Takeaways: Timed motion and independent stopping
  • Prediction is not execution authority: A long predicted path can guide motion, but the controller executes only short, validated prefixes. While renewal cadence dictates how frequently new plans are requested, target evidence retains its own capture-based timestamp, and its evidence horizon bounds how long it stays valid.
  • Reserve a stopping response from the current state: A stopping suffix is mathematically valid only for its declared entry state. If an execution lease or sensor observation expires, the machine must possess a pre-validated fallback trajectory, sufficient reserve energy, and enough physical clearance to absorb both response latency and full deceleration.
  • Admission precedes physical commitment: The alternate arm schedule closes replacement admission strictly by 278 ms to ensure a physical stop can initiate at 300 ms. A late planner proposal cannot spend or delay this reserved response window.
  • A collision-free path is not inherently executable: Near singularities, Cartesian end-effector speeds demand infeasible joint velocities. Damped least squares bounds the inverse-kinematics step only by introducing tracking error. Similarly, time-optimal path timing produces discontinuous torque demands that trigger actuator saturation. Adding quintic seams and jerk bounds smooths transitions, but interior joint limits, inverter voltage headroom, and clearance remain binding constraints.
  • Trajectory smoothness dictates stopping distance: Finite jerk bounds make smooth stopping profiles significantly longer than ideal constant-deceleration braking. An S-curve profile pays the least, the equivalent of cruising for half of one jerk ramp, yet that overhead still sets operating speed limits near rack ends and obstacles.
  • Candidate rejection does not require immediate stopping: The running trajectory continues executing normally as long as its state, sensor evidence, lease deadline, and fallback trajectory remain valid. Rejecting an invalid replacement proposal simply leaves those original expiration boundaries unchanged.
  • Admission and runtime enforcement require separate system budgets: Evaluating a candidate plan requires an asynchronous, bounded scan over the entire trajectory chunk, whereas per-tick enforcement performs strict real-time checks against current state and local sensor data. System designers must isolate their CPU time, memory footprints, and bus interference to prevent high-level planning from disrupting the low-level control loop.

A plan stays valid only while the stop it carries can still be executed. When a timing, seam, or clearance check fails, the remedy is to shorten the lease, slow the motion, or lengthen the bridge until every check passes with the resident stop still inside the clearance, never to wait for a better proposal. The stall runway shows which term to work on. If the travel before stop onset rivals the stop itself, the lease is the term to shorten. If the stop dominates, the deceleration or jerk bound the load tolerates is the term to measure.

For motion, the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), reduces to a clearance test. However smooth, collision-free, and timely a candidate is, the MCU admits it only with an independently executable stop that fits the clearance that remains, and that test does not depend on how the candidate was generated. Admission is not a waiver either. The conditions certified at admission remain obligations on every control tick, so the machine must keep checking live state against the clearance it has left.

11.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Motion planning became a concrete computational discipline when Lozano-Pérez (1983) mapped geometric collision avoidance into configuration-space search, shrinking the geometry of a complex moving body to a single point. In the same year, Schwartz and Sharir (1983) formulated the piano movers’ problem through exact algebraic decomposition. While theoretically complete, exact algebraic methods scale doubly exponentially with degrees of freedom, rendering them intractable for multi-joint manipulators. In the 1990s, randomized sampling bypassed this geometric bottleneck by trading exact completeness for probabilistic guarantees. Probabilistic roadmaps (Kavraki et al. 1996) introduced multi-query graph construction across free configuration space, while rapidly-exploring random trees (LaValle 1998) solved single-query kinodynamic problems by incrementally biasing search trees toward unexplored regions. Later, RRT* (Karaman and Frazzoli 2011) proved that standard random trees almost surely fail to find optimal paths, introducing local edge rewiring to guarantee asymptotic convergence toward the optimum. Together, these foundational algorithms resolve where a robot may safely pass, which represents the geometric half of motion planning.

Kavraki, Lydia E., Petr Švestka, Jean-Claude Latombe, and Mark H. Overmars. 1996. “Probabilistic Roadmaps for Path Planning in High-Dimensional Configuration Spaces.” IEEE Transactions on Robotics and Automation 12 (4): 566–80. https://doi.org/10.1109/70.508439.
LaValle, Steven M. 1998. Rapidly-Exploring Random Trees: A New Tool for Path Planning. TR 98-11. Computer Science Department, Iowa State University.
Karaman, Sertac, and Emilio Frazzoli. 2011. “Sampling-Based Algorithms for Optimal Motion Planning.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 30 (7): 846–94. https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364911406761.
Bobrow, James E., Steven Dubowsky, and J. S. Gibson. 1985. “Time-Optimal Control of Robotic Manipulators Along Specified Paths.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 4 (3): 3–17. https://doi.org/10.1177/027836498500400301.
Pham, Hung, and Quang-Cuong Pham. 2018. “A New Approach to Time-Optimal Path Parameterization Based on Reachability Analysis.” IEEE Transactions on Robotics 34 (3): 645–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/TRO.2018.2819195.
Berscheid, Lars, and Torsten Kröger. 2021. “Jerk-Limited Real-Time Trajectory Generation with Arbitrary Target States.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS) XVII. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2021.XVII.015.
Ratliff, Nathan, Matt Zucker, J. Andrew Bagnell, and Siddhartha Srinivasa. 2009. “CHOMP: Gradient Optimization Techniques for Efficient Motion Planning.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 489–94. https://doi.org/10.1109/ROBOT.2009.5152817.

The second half, timing and dynamics, followed an independent line of work. Bobrow et al. (1985) showed that the fastest traversal along a pre-specified path under actuator torque limits follows a bang-bang structure in the phase plane, switching between maximum acceleration and maximum deceleration. Decades later, TOPP-RA (Pham and Pham 2018) reformulated time-optimal path parameterization through reachability analysis, casting the search into a sequence of linear programs that execute in time linear in the number of path waypoints. Pure time-optimal trajectories command discontinuous accelerations, however, and the resulting infinite jerk excites mechanical resonances that degrade physical drives. To produce smoother execution, roboticists drew inspiration from biology: Flash and Hogan (1985) showed that human reaching movements naturally minimize jerk. In robotic manipulation, Kröger and Wahl (2010) demonstrated that complete, jerk-limited trajectory profiles could be synthesized deterministically from an arbitrary initial state within a single millisecond-scale control cycle. Ruckig (Berscheid and Kröger 2021) generalized this formulation to arbitrary target kinematic states and asymmetric limits, establishing the fast, closed-form generator that produces this chapter’s stopping profiles. Trajectory optimization reached robotics along a parallel route. Direct collocation (Hargraves and Paris 1987) originated in aerospace trajectory design to transcribe continuous optimal control into sparse nonlinear programs. Receding-horizon control matured in the process industries (Qin and Badgwell 2003), where slow chemical plant dynamics afforded ample time for numerical convergence. Later, algorithms like CHOMP (Ratliff et al. 2009) brought functional gradient optimization directly to robotic arms, optimizing trajectory smoothness and obstacle clearance simultaneously.

The commitment problem, deciding when a planned motion becomes irrevocable and keeping execution safe after that point, is the most recent to mature. Because physical systems possess inertia, safety cannot be judged solely by examining whether the current state is collision-free. Fraichard and Asama (2004) formalized inevitable collision states, establishing that a planner must verify that a system never enters a state from which every future control trajectory culminates in a crash. This formulation gave a robotic form to the viability kernels that Aubin (1991) had introduced in control theory. Modern learning-based methods have transformed how candidate motions are generated. Action chunking (Zhao et al. 2023) and diffusion policies (Chi et al. 2025) predict multi-step action trajectories directly from visual observations, capturing dexterous behaviors without explicit geometric reconstruction. To bridge the latency gap between neural network inference and high-frequency actuation, real-time chunking (Black et al. 2025) conditions incoming predictions on the action sequence already committed to the hardware controller. Regardless of how candidate motions are generated, the execution runtime must enforce physical safety. The trajectory record binds the admitted trajectory to its initial seam state, enforces hard commitment deadlines, and guarantees a deterministically executable stopping suffix whenever the policy stalls.

Zhao, Tony Z., Vikash Kumar, Sergey Levine, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation with Low-Cost Hardware.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.016.

For further reading, six works trace the lineage from classical planning to modern learned proposals:

  • Qin and Badgwell (2003) provides a comprehensive survey of industrial model predictive control, charting its progression from early petrochemical applications to commercial implementations and illustrating how receding-horizon optimization operates on physical systems whose long time constants tolerate multi-second solve times.
  • Fraichard and Asama (2004) formalizes inevitable collision states and defines the dynamic conditions required for provable motion safety under actuator limits, establishing the theoretical foundation for the commitment deadlines analyzed in section 11.5.3.
  • Biagiotti and Melchiorri (2008) provides an exhaustive reference on trajectory generation profiles across robotics and industrial automation, detailing the analytical formulation of polynomial splines, filter-based smoothers, and the double-S profile used for the \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stopping suffix in section 11.5.2.
  • Kröger and Wahl (2010) develops the mathematical framework for online trajectory generation, deriving algorithms that compute kinematically feasible, jerk-limited motion profiles from arbitrary initial states within single-cycle real-time deadlines.
  • Lynch and Park (2017) offers a unified, geometrically rigorous treatment of configuration space, kinematics, rigid-body dynamics, time scaling, and motion planning, serving as a comprehensive companion to Forward kinematics and singularities.
  • Chi et al. (2025) details diffusion policies for robotic manipulation, examining receding-horizon execution of predicted action sequences and presenting the modern learned proposers whose seams, deadlines, and fallback stopping profiles this chapter evaluates.
Fraichard, Thierry, and Hajime Asama. 2004. “Inevitable Collision States: A Step Towards Safer Robots?” Advanced Robotics 18 (10): 1001–24. https://doi.org/10.1163/1568553042674662.
Biagiotti, Luigi, and Claudio Melchiorri. 2008. Trajectory Planning for Automatic Machines and Robots. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-85629-0.
Kröger, Torsten, and Friedrich M. Wahl. 2010. “Online Trajectory Generation: Basic Concepts for Instantaneous Reactions to Unforeseen Events.” IEEE Transactions on Robotics 26 (1): 94–111. https://doi.org/10.1109/TRO.2009.2035744.
Lynch, Kevin M, and Frank C Park. 2017. Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control. Cambridge University Press.
Chi, Cheng, Zhenjia Xu, Siyuan Feng, Eric Cousineau, Yilun Du, Benjamin Burchfiel, Russ Tedrake, and Shuran Song. 2025. “Diffusion Policy: Visuomotor Policy Learning via Action Diffusion.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 44 (10-11): 1684–704. https://doi.org/10.1177/02783649241273668.

What’s Next: From admitted plans to per-tick permission
Admission judges a trajectory against the state the planner predicted, but the machine executes it against the state it actually reaches. Between the two, the body lags its reference, a person can step into the aisle, and an actuator can run short of torque, and the stopping budget assembled here has not yet charged the first of these. On the base the gap is already decisive, because the jerk-limited stop carries the partial budget to 1,123.84 mm against 1.10 m of rack-end clearance at the drive limit before the body’s departure from its reference is priced. Safety Enforcement begins with the per-tick gatekeeper, the permission-path check that runs on every control tick against live state. It prices tracking error as an inset on the stopping distance and derives a candidate aisle speed whose timing and braking premises still require qualification. When a check fails, it selects among fallback responses that include the resident stopping suffix latched at admission.
